113	fn pick_inner(step_cost_ns: f64, framing_ns: f64) -> u64 {
114	    let target = (FRAMING_DOMINATION_RATIO * framing_ns / step_cost_ns).ceil() as u64;
115	    target.clamp(1, MAX_INNER)
116	}
117	
118	fn run_counted<B: Bench>(bench: &mut B, outer: u64, inner: u64) -> (Histogram<u64>, f64) {
119	    let mut hist = new_hist();
120	    let run_start = minstant::Instant::now();
121	    for _ in 0..outer {
122	        record_sample(bench, inner, &mut hist);
123	    }
124	    let duration_s = run_start.elapsed().as_nanos() as f64 / 1e9;
125	    (hist, duration_s)
126	}
127	
128	fn run_timed<B: Bench>(bench: &mut B, target_seconds: f64, inner: u64) -> (Histogram<u64>, f64) {
129	    let mut hist = new_hist();
130	    let target_ns = (target_seconds * 1e9) as u128;
131	    let run_start = minstant::Instant::now();
132	    loop {
133	        record_sample(bench, inner, &mut hist);
134	        if run_start.elapsed().as_nanos() >= target_ns {
135	            break;
136	        }
137	    }
138	    let duration_s = run_start.elapsed().as_nanos() as f64 / 1e9;
139	    (hist, duration_s)
140	}
141	
142	fn new_hist() -> Histogram<u64> {
143	    // 1 ns to 60 s, 3 sig figs
144	    Histogram::<u64>::new_with_bounds(1, 60_000_000_000, 3).unwrap()
145	}
146	
147	fn record_sample<B: Bench>(bench: &mut B, inner: u64, hist: &mut Histogram<u64>) {
148	    let start = minstant::Instant::now();
149	    for _ in 0..inner {
150	        black_box(bench.step());
151	    }
152	    let elapsed_ns = start.elapsed().as_nanos() as u64;
153	    hist.record(round_elapsed(elapsed_ns, inner)).unwrap();
154	}
155	
156	fn round_elapsed(elapsed_ns: u64, inner: u64) -> u64 {
157	    (elapsed_ns + inner / 2) / inner
158	}
159	
160	/// Format an integer with thousands separators, e.g.
161	/// `12345` → `"12,345"`.
162	pub fn fmt_commas(n: u64) -> String {
163	    let s = n.to_string();
164	    let mut result = String::new();
165	    for (i, c) in s.chars().rev().enumerate() {
166	        if i > 0 && i % 3 == 0 {
167	            result.push(',');
168	        }
169	        result.push(c);
170	    }
171	    result.chars().rev().collect()
172	}
173	
174	/// Format a float with `decimals` fractional digits and thousands
175	/// separators on the integer part.
176	pub fn fmt_commas_f64(n: f64, decimals: usize) -> String {
177	    let s = format!("{n:.decimals$}");
178	    let (sign, body) = match s.strip_prefix('-') {
179	        Some(rest) => ("-", rest),
180	        None => ("", s.as_str()),
181	    };
182	    let (int_part, frac_part) = match body.find('.') {
183	        Some(i) => (&body[..i], &body[i..]),
184	        None => (body, ""),
185	    };
186	    let int_num: u64 = int_part.parse().unwrap_or(0);
187	    format!("{sign}{}{frac_part}", fmt_commas(int_num))
188	}

0	//! Generic bench driver: the [`Bench`] trait, adaptive outer/inner
1	//! loop sizing, and the band-histogram report.
2	
3	use std::hint::black_box;
4	
5	use hdrhistogram::Histogram;
6	
7	use crate::overhead::Overhead;
8	
9	const WARMUP: u64 = 10_000;
10	const ESTIMATE_STEPS: u64 = 1_000;
11	const ESTIMATE_SAMPLES: usize = 5;
12	const FRAMING_DOMINATION_RATIO: f64 = 10.0;
13	const MAX_INNER: u64 = 1_000;
14	
15	/// A benchmark workload: a named operation that `step()` performs
16	/// in a tight loop for sub-µs latency measurement. Implementors own
17	/// any setup state (channels, spawned threads, counters). `step()`
18	/// returns a value so the caller can `black_box` it against dead-code
19	/// elimination.
20	pub trait Bench {
21	    /// Human-readable name used in the report header.
22	    fn name(&self) -> &str;
23	
24	    /// Run one unit of work. Return any value derived from the work
25	    /// to defeat DCE — the caller black-boxes it.
26	    fn step(&mut self) -> u64;
27	}
28	
29	/// Runtime configuration for one [`run_adaptive`] call.
30	#[derive(Debug)]
31	pub struct RunCfg<'a> {
32	    /// Calibrated apparatus overhead (framing + loop/iter) used to
33	    /// compute the `adjusted` mean columns in the report.
34	    pub overhead: &'a Overhead,
35	    /// Wall-clock seconds budget for time-based runs. Ignored when
36	    /// `outer_override` is set.
37	    pub target_seconds: f64,
38	    /// Force a fixed outer-loop count, bypassing the time budget.
39	    pub outer_override: Option<u64>,
40	    /// Force a fixed inner-loop count, bypassing the
41	    /// overhead-dominated auto-sizing.
42	    pub inner_override: Option<u64>,
43	    /// Core pool for thread pinning. Indexed positionally with
44	    /// wrap-around via [`core_for`][RunCfg::core_for]; empty means
45	    /// no pinning.
46	    pub pin_cores: &'a [usize],
47	    /// When set, [`crate::tprobe::TProbe::report`] emits raw TSC
48	    /// ticks instead of nanoseconds. Plumbed from the `-t/--ticks`
49	    /// CLI flag.
50	    pub report_ticks: bool,
51	}
52	
53	impl RunCfg<'_> {
54	    /// CPU id for the bench's `thread_idx`-th thread, using
55	    /// wrap-around over the pool. Returns `None` when the pool is
56	    /// empty so callers can treat unpinned and pinned runs uniformly.
57	    pub fn core_for(&self, thread_idx: usize) -> Option<usize> {
58	        if self.pin_cores.is_empty() {
59	            None
60	        } else {
61	            Some(self.pin_cores[thread_idx % self.pin_cores.len()])
62	        }
63	    }
64	}
65	
66	/// Drive `bench` against `cfg` and return
67	/// `(histogram, outer, inner, duration_s)`.
68	///
69	/// After a fixed warmup, `inner` is auto-sized so apparatus framing
70	/// doesn't dominate (skipped when `cfg.inner_override` is set). The
71	/// outer loop runs either for `cfg.outer_override` iterations or
72	/// until `cfg.target_seconds` elapses.
73	pub fn run_adaptive<B: Bench>(bench: &mut B, cfg: &RunCfg) -> (Histogram<u64>, u64, u64, f64) {
74	    for _ in 0..WARMUP {
75	        black_box(bench.step());
76	    }
77	
78	    let step_cost_ns = estimate_step_cost(bench);
79	    let framing_ns = cfg.overhead.framing_per_sample_ns.max(1.0);
80	    let inner = cfg
81	        .inner_override
82	        .unwrap_or_else(|| pick_inner(step_cost_ns, framing_ns));
83	
84	    match cfg.outer_override {
85	        Some(outer) => {
86	            let (hist, duration_s) = run_counted(bench, outer, inner);
87	            (hist, outer, inner, duration_s)
88	        }
89	        None => {
90	            let (hist, duration_s) = run_timed(bench, cfg.target_seconds, inner);
91	            let outer = hist.len();
92	            (hist, outer, inner, duration_s)
93	        }
94	    }
95	}
96	
97	fn estimate_step_cost<B: Bench>(bench: &mut B) -> f64 {
98	    let mut samples: Vec<f64> = (0..ESTIMATE_SAMPLES)
99	        .map(|_| {
```

1	//! Shared setup for the `zcr-*` benches: leaked ring regions
2	//! and `'static` endpoint construction over the sibling
3	//! `zc-ring-x1` crate.
4	
5	use zc_ring_x1::{CACHE_LINE_SIZE, Consumer, Header, Producer, Ring};
6	
7	/// Slot payload for every zcr bench: the round-trip counter.
8	/// `u64` satisfies the zerocopy bounds and matches the message
9	/// shape of the mpsc/ice benches.
10	pub type Msg = u64;
11	
12	/// Shutdown sentinel the 2t benches send instead of a counter
13	/// value; the echo worker exits on receipt without replying.
14	/// The counter increments skip it (see each bench's `step`).
15	pub const STOP: Msg = u64::MAX;
16	
17	/// Slots per ring — a power of two, comfortably above the one
18	/// message ever in flight in the round-trip benches.
19	pub const CAPACITY: u32 = 8;
20	
21	/// Region bytes: the four-cache-line [`Header`] plus
22	/// [`CAPACITY`] slots of one cache line each.
23	const REGION_BYTES: usize = size_of::<Header>() + CACHE_LINE_SIZE * CAPACITY as usize;
24	
25	/// Cache-line-aligned backing region for one ring, matching
26	/// `Ring::init`'s alignment requirement.
27	#[repr(C, align(64))]
28	struct Region([u8; REGION_BYTES]);
29	
30	/// Build a ring over a leaked region and split it into
31	/// `'static` endpoint handles.
32	///
33	/// - Leaked on purpose: the 2t benches move one endpoint into a
34	///   spawned worker thread, so the region must outlive the
35	///   bench struct. ~768 B per ring for the process lifetime is
36	///   fine in a bench binary.
37	pub fn leak_ring() -> (Producer<'static>, Consumer<'static>) {
38	    let region: &'static mut Region = Box::leak(Box::new(Region([0; REGION_BYTES])));
39	    Ring::init(&mut region.0, CACHE_LINE_SIZE as u32, CAPACITY)
40	        .expect("geometry is valid by construction")
41	        .split()
42	}
43	
```

1	//! Two-threaded zc-ring-x1 round-trip bench, easy
2	//! (`reserve_slot_spin`) API tier.
3	
4	use std::hint::black_box;
5	use std::thread;
6	
7	use zc_ring_x1::{Consumer, Producer};
8	
9	use crate::benches::zcr_common::{Msg, STOP, leak_ring};
10	use crate::harness::{self, Bench, RunCfg};
11	use crate::pin;
12	
13	/// Registry name used on the CLI.
14	pub const NAME: &str = "zcr-spin-2t";
15	
16	/// Main → worker → main round-trip over two zc-ring-x1 rings as
17	/// `zcr-raw-2t`, but both ends wait inside `reserve_slot_spin` —
18	/// the easy tier with the spin policy baked in.
19	///
20	/// - Same effective wait policy as `zcr-raw-2t` / `zcr-with-2t`
21	///   (spin_loop hint per failed attempt), so any delta between
22	///   the three is pure API-tier cost under cross-core traffic.
23	/// - Shutdown: `Drop` sends the [`STOP`] sentinel; the worker
24	///   exits on receipt without replying. `reserve_slot_spin`
25	///   never gives up, so the sentinel (not a closure returning
26	///   `false`) is the only way to unblock a spinning consumer.
27	pub struct ZcrSpin2Thread {
28	    req_tx: Producer<'static>,
29	    resp_rx: Consumer<'static>,
30	    worker: Option<thread::JoinHandle<()>>,
31	    counter: u64,
32	}
33	
34	impl ZcrSpin2Thread {
35	    /// Spawn the spinning echo worker over two fresh leaked
36	    /// rings, optionally pinning it to `worker_cpu`.
37	    pub fn new(worker_cpu: Option<usize>) -> Self {
38	        let (req_tx, mut req_rx) = leak_ring();
39	        let (mut resp_tx, resp_rx) = leak_ring();
40	        let worker = thread::spawn(move || {
41	            pin::pin_current(worker_cpu);
42	            loop {
43	                let slot = req_rx.reserve_slot_spin::<Msg>();
44	                let v = *slot;
45	                slot.release();
46	                if v == STOP {
47	                    break;
48	                }
49	                let mut slot = resp_tx.reserve_slot_spin::<Msg>();
50	                *slot = v;
51	                slot.commit();
52	            }
53	        });
54	        Self {
55	            req_tx,
56	            resp_rx,
57	            worker: Some(worker),
58	            counter: 0,
59	        }
60	    }
61	}
62	
63	impl Bench for ZcrSpin2Thread {
64	    fn name(&self) -> &str {
65	        "zcr-spin-2t: zc-ring-x1 reserve_slot_spin round-trip (2 threads, spin)"
66	    }
67	
68	    fn step(&mut self) -> u64 {
69	        self.counter = self.counter.wrapping_add(1);
70	        if self.counter == STOP {
71	            self.counter = 1;
72	        }
73	        let mut slot = self.req_tx.reserve_slot_spin::<Msg>();
74	        *slot = self.counter;
75	        slot.commit();
76	        let slot = self.resp_rx.reserve_slot_spin::<Msg>();
77	        let v = *slot;
78	        slot.release();
79	        black_box(v)
80	    }
81	}
82	
83	impl Drop for ZcrSpin2Thread {
84	    /// Send [`STOP`] and join the worker.
85	    fn drop(&mut self) {
86	        let mut slot = self.req_tx.reserve_slot_spin::<Msg>();
87	        *slot = STOP;
88	        slot.commit();
89	        if let Some(worker) = self.worker.take() {
90	            let _ = worker.join();
91	        }
92	    }
93	}
94	
95	/// Registry entry point.
96	pub fn run(cfg: &RunCfg) {
97	    let mut bench = ZcrSpin2Thread::new(cfg.core_for(1));
98	    let (hist, outer, inner, duration_s) = harness::run_adaptive(&mut bench, cfg);
99	    harness::print_report(bench.name(), outer, inner, duration_s, &hist, cfg.overhead);
100	}
101	
```

> AGENT

Root cause found, and it's not a u64 overflow — your machine **suspended mid-run**, and the panic is the histogram rejecting a sample that spanned the sleep.

## The panic

`new_hist()` at `src/harness.rs:144` builds the histogram with bounds 1 ns – 60 s and auto-resize disabled. `hist.record(...).unwrap()` at `src/harness.rs:153` panics with `ValueOutOfRangeResizeDisabled` the moment a single recorded value exceeds 60 seconds. A u64 holds ~584 years of nanoseconds, so overflow was never in play.

## Why samples exceeded 60 s

The journal confirms the system slept during both runs, and each panic lines up with a resume to the second:

- **600 s run**: `PM: suspend exit` at **07:06:30**; your panic prompt printed at **07:06:28.6**. The machine went to sleep while `zcr-spin-2t` was mid-sample; on wake, that sample's elapsed included the whole sleep gap, blew past 60 s, and the record call panicked.
- **300 s run**: suspend entry at **09:50:47**, exit at **13:21:25**; panic prompt at **13:21:23.6**. Same story with `zcr-raw-2t` — it slept 3.5 hours mid-sample and panicked immediately on wake.

The journal shows an idle-suspend pattern all morning (entries at 07:55, 09:01, 09:50 — roughly 40 min after each resume), so KDE's power management is putting the box to sleep while benches run unattended. We think minstant's TSC keeps counting across s2idle on this machine, which is why the sleep gap shows up in the measurement at all.

This also explains the two other anomalies you flagged:

- **`zcr-with-1t`'s 40.6 s max and 1658.7 s duration**: the recorded value is per-call (`elapsed / inner`, inner=33), so 40.6 s recorded = a raw sample of ~1,340 s ≈ a 22-minute sleep mid-sample. 600 s target + ~1,050 s of sleep ≈ the 1658.7 s reported duration. Consistent.
- **Why only 2t benches panic**: they run with `inner=1`, so any sleep > 60 s records raw and overflows. The 1t benches divide by ~33, so sleeps up to ~33 minutes still squeeze under the 60 s cap — they get silently poisoned (that 40.6 s max, the 1 ms stdev) instead of panicking.

The ordinary p99-max tails — 14–300 µs on 1t, 1.9–3 ms on 2t — are just scheduler preemption on an unpinned run; those look normal. And your trimmed `mean min-p99` stats already shrug all of this off: every zcr-1t tier reads 4 ns adjusted, raw-2t 112 ns, with-2t 97 ns.

## What I'd suggest

Two independent fixes:

1. **Harness**: replace `hist.record(v).unwrap()` with `hist.saturating_record(v)` — clamps at the 60 s bound, never panics, memory stays bounded. A ten-hour-old run should never die on the last bench because the machine napped.
2. **Operational**: run long benches under `systemd-inhibit --what=sleep iiac-perf ...` so the box can't suspend mid-run — that's the fix for data quality; saturating only fixes the crash, a sleep still poisons mean/stdev for that bench.

Want me to start a cycle for the `saturating_record` change (and perhaps a note in the docs about `systemd-inhibit` for long runs)?

> DEVELOPER

I was guessing the computer was sleeping, great you could confirm it! Will the `systemd-inhibit ...` eliminate those issues, if I have a server how is this handle, in that case you "never" want the computer to sleep?

> DEVELOPER

Great you found it, will the `systemd-inhibit ..`, I assume when iiac-perf stops it will sleep. If this was a server, which should never sleep, how is this handled?

> AGENT

Yes, exactly right. `systemd-inhibit --what=sleep iiac-perf ...` takes an inhibitor lock for the lifetime of the child process — when `iiac-perf` exits (or crashes), the lock is released and your normal idle-suspend policy resumes. It's scoped protection: nothing to remember to undo, and a dead bench never leaves the machine insomniac. One nuance: `--what=sleep` blocks the *system* from suspending, but by default powerdevil's idle timer is what initiates the sleep — the inhibitor makes that request fail (or with `--what=idle:sleep` it never fires). Either way the box stays awake exactly as long as the bench runs.

For a server, the answer is: this problem is configured out of existence, not inhibited at runtime. A few layers, in the order they're usually applied:

- **No policy asks for sleep.** Your desktop sleeps because KDE's powerdevil has an idle timeout. Servers run headless with no desktop power manager, and `systemd-logind`'s own idle action defaults to none (`IdleAction=ignore` in `/etc/systemd/logind.conf`). Nothing ever *requests* suspend, so nothing needs to block it.
- **Sleep is masked outright.** Belt-and-suspenders on many server images:
  `systemctl mask sleep.target suspend.target hibernate.target hybrid-sleep.target`
  After that, even an explicit `systemctl suspend` fails. This is the canonical "this machine must never sleep" switch.
- **logind is told to ignore triggers.** For hardware that has lid switches or power buttons (laptops drafted as servers), `HandleLidSwitch=ignore`, `HandleSuspendKey=ignore` in `logind.conf`.
- **Firmware level.** Server BIOS/BMC platforms typically don't expose S3/s2idle at all — the deepest ACPI sleep state offered is often just soft-off. Rack machines are expected to idle in C-states (per-core sleep, which doesn't stop the clocks or the TSC in any way that hurts you) rather than system suspend.

So the desktop model is "sleep by default, inhibit for exceptions" and the server model is "never sleep, full stop" — `systemd-inhibit` is the right tool for your 3900X precisely because it's a desktop you presumably *do* want sleeping when it's not benching.

If you'd rather make this machine bench-safe permanently instead of wrapping each run, the middle option is masking the sleep targets and re-enabling them when you want them — but the wrapper is the cleaner fit for a dual-use desktop. And on the harness side, `saturating_record` is still worth doing regardless: it also guards against any legitimately-slow >60 s sample, not just suspend artifacts. Want me to start that cycle?

1	# Cycle protocol
2	
3	This protocol uses [Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form). It
4	contains instructions on how a commit cycle is accomplished.
5	
6	The artifact a cycle produces is whatever the bot generates from
7	the conversation — code, prose, an image, a song, a screenplay.
8	The steps below use a Rust crate as the running example (the
9	cargo cycle, `Cargo.toml` versioning); substitute your medium's
10	equivalents — this project's manifest is recorded in
11	[versioning.md](versioning.md).
12	
13	## Cycles
14	
15	A cycle has three phases:
16	
17	- **[Preparation](#preparation)** (`X.Y.Z-0`) — the cycle's
18	  first commit, when it needs setup (a lightweight cycle omits
19	  it and starts at `-1` — see
20	  [versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)). Sets up the
21	  cycle:
22	  - Bump the version-of-record to `X.Y.Z-0` (where it lives
23	    and the suffix scheme are project-specific — see
24	    [versioning.md](versioning.md)).
25	  - Pick up a `## Todo` item (typically the top-ranked,
26	    #1) into `## In Progress` (bold title + succinct problem
27	    statement + plan ladder).
28	  - Open the [chores section](#chores-sections).
29	- **[Work-N](#work-n)** (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …) — the
30	  commits that implement the change. As many as the change
31	  needs; each runs through the
32	  [per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow).
33	- **[Close-out](#close-out)** (bare `X.Y.Z`) — the cycle's
34	  last commit. Bookkeeping only:
35	  - Move the picked-up item to a one-line entry in
36	    `## Done`.
37	  - Move the `## In Progress` block into the
38	    [chores section](#chores-sections).
39	  - Optionally update `notes/README.md` if functionality
40	    changed.
41	
42	A cycle's commits are published to the project remote
43	either incrementally or as one batch at close-out; the
44	result must always be published at close-out. See
45	[Pushing](#pushing).
46	
47	**Sub-cycles.** When a Work commit's scope grows enough to
48	warrant its own ladder, it subdivides — `X.Y.Z-3.0`
49	Preparation, `X.Y.Z-3.1` / `X.Y.Z-3.2` Work, `X.Y.Z-3`
50	Close-out. The same three-phase shape applies recursively
51	at every depth. See [Numbering](#numbering) for the
52	suffix rule and [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)
53	for the local-ladder mechanics.
54	
55	## Chores sections
56	
57	A **chores section** is a `##` section in
58	`notes/chores/chores-NN.md` recording landed work. In
59	general, every commit that lands on the permanent branch
60	should have a reference to it on a `Commits:` list in a
61	chores file.
62	
63	The phrase **"Open" the chores section** means append a
64	`##` header to the current `notes/chores/chores-NN.md`
65	with the title it records (e.g. `## refactor: foo bar`),
66	followed by an **empty `Commits:`** line. The narrative
67	body stays empty until close-out (see [Close-out](#close-out));
68	the `Commits:` line is backfilled later, once the commit is
69	permanent (see [Commits backfill](#commits-backfill) below).
70	
71	Fuller chores conventions (content rules, header sync,
72	design subsection pattern, `Commits:` formatting) live in
73	AGENTS.md [Chores conventions](../AGENTS.md#chores-conventions).
74	
75	### Commits backfill
76	
77	A chores section's `Commits:` line cites the commit(s) it
78	records, by SHA — but a SHA isn't stable until the commit
79	lands on a **permanent branch** (`main`, or a long-lived
80	release/patch branch that won't be rewritten); a rebase or
81	squash rewrites it on the way. So:
82	
83	- A chores section is **opened with an empty `Commits:`** line.
84	- **Backfill once the commit is on a permanent branch**,
85	  where its SHA is final. A commit can't record its own SHA
86	  (that would change the hash), so the fill always lands one
87	  push later: **each push backfills the `Commits:` of the
88	  commits the previous push made permanent.** On a topic
89	  branch the sections instead wait until the branch lands —
90	  so no SHA is ever written that a later rebase could
91	  invalidate.
92	
93	Use `[[N]]` refs — several as `[[N]],[[M]]` only when one
94	section records multiple commits (a merge non-ff close-out) —
95	with the commit URL + 40-hex SHA in the file's `# References`
96	(format in AGENTS.md
97	[Chores commit references](../AGENTS.md#chores-commit-references)).
98	A section's `##` title matches its commit title, so a rare
99	deliberate rewrite of a permanent-branch commit re-syncs via
100	`git log --grep "<title>"`.
101	
102	The per-push cadence is a project choice, not dogma — a
103	**per-close-out** model (recording a cycle's SHAs at its
104	close-out) is equally valid. The one invariant: a recorded
105	SHA must be permanent.
106	
107	## Preparation
108	
109	The cycle's first commit (`X.Y.Z-0`), when the cycle needs
110	setup (a lightweight cycle omits it — see
111	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)):
112	
113	- **Bump the version-of-record** to `X.Y.Z-0`. Where it
114	  lives, the suffix scheme, and any derived files (a
115	  lockfile, a sourced manifest version) are
116	  project-specific — see [versioning.md](versioning.md).
117	- **Move a `## Todo` item** (if the cycle has one) into
118	  `## In Progress` and the todo item should have:
119	  - A **bold title line** — that will be the chores
120	    section header, minus the `## ` prefix.
121	  - A **succinct problem statement**; add if one is needed
122	  - A **plan ladder**.
123	- **Open the [chores section](#chores-sections)** —
124	  append a `##` header with the title it records — the
125	  cycle's anticipated close-out title.
126	
127	## Work-N
128	
129	The cycle's work commits (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …)
130	implement the change. As many as needed:
131	
132	- Each commit runs through the
133	  **[per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow)**.
134	- **Interim pushes** are optional (backup, progress
135	  visibility).
136	- Close-out is the only mandatory push (see
137	  [Pushing](#pushing)).
138	- **Subdivide into a sub-cycle** if a Work commit's
139	  scope grows enough (see
140	  [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)).
141	
142	## Close-out
143	
144	The cycle's last commit (bare `X.Y.Z`) does bookkeeping
145	only — the commit body describes that bookkeeping, not
146	what happens post-squash:
147	
148	- **Move the picked-up item** from `## In Progress` to a
149	  one-line entry (with a chores `[N]` ref) in `## Done`.
150	- **Move the `## In Progress` block into the
151	  [chores section](#chores-sections)** (the title-only
152	  placeholder opened during Preparation):
153	  - Cut the problem statement + plan ladder out of
154	    `## In Progress` (drop the bold title line — the
155	    chores `##` header already carries that string).
156	  - Paste under the chores `##` header.
157	  - Sync the chores header to the **final** commit title
158	    if the cycle's scope shifted; update every anchor
159	    back-reference.
160	  - Add an `### As-built ladder` listing the cycle's
161	    commits; optional `### Outcome` notes.
162	  - Replace `## In Progress` with
163	    `_No cycle currently in progress._`.
164	- **Update `notes/README.md`** if functionality changed
165	  (new flags, new subcommands, changed behavior).
166	
167	Whether to **squash** the cycle into one commit before the
168	publishing push, or push as-is, is decided at push time —
169	see [Pushing](#pushing).
170	
171	## Numbering
172	
173	Each commit's phase is encoded in the version suffix — `-0`
174	Preparation, `-1`/`-2`/… Work, bare `X.Y.Z` Close-out,
175	recursively for sub-cycles. The full scheme — disambiguation,
176	nesting, optional Preparation, the project's version-of-record
177	format, and the per-phase bump — lives in
178	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering), which is the
179	single source of truth for this repo's versioning.
180	
181	## Per-commit flow
182	
183	Every commit (Preparation, each Work commit, Close-out) goes
184	through:
185	
186	1. **Mark this commit `(current)`** as the first edit in
187	   `notes/todo.md > ## In Progress`.
188	2. **Do the work** (see [Iterative work](#iterative-work)
189	   for the loop-and-squash technique).
190	3. **Flip this commit `(current)` → `(done)`** in `## In
191	   Progress` — before the cargo cycle and the commit.
192	4. **Validate the artifact** — a medium-specific step, skip-able
193	   for notes-only commits, mandatory at close-out. For the Rust
194	   example the cargo cycle is:
195	   1. `cargo fmt`
196	   2. `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`
197	   3. `cargo test`
198	   4. `cargo install --path . --locked`
199	   5. (re-test if anything substantive changed)
200	5. **Work review.** Stop *before* writing any description;
201	   tell the user "ready to commit." The user reviews the
202	   changes and we iterate until complete.
203	6. **Write the commit description** — see
204	   [Commit description](#commit-description).
205	7. **Commit Description review.** Show the title + body
206	   and stop. The user reviews the description. Iterate.
207	8. **Commit.** `jj commit -m "title" -m "body" -R .` for
208	   the app repo, `-R .claude` for the bot repo (`-R` last
209	   keeps the verb visible):
210	
211	   ```
212	   jj commit -m \
213	   "<type>: <short description>" \
214	   -m "<intro paragraph>
215	
216	   - file1: gist
217	   - file2: gist
218	
219	   ochid: /.claude/<chid>" \
220	   -R .
221	   ```
222	
223	**Two overrides apply:**
224	
225	- **Deviation or question** — any time the work deviates
226	  from the agreed plan, or a question arises, stop and
227	  surface it; don't push through.
228	- **ESC-ESC** — the user can interrupt at any point to pull
229	  a review or question forward.
230	
231	## Commit description
232	
233	[Conventional Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/):
234	
235	```
236	<type>: <short description>
237	```
238	
239	Titles carry **no trailing `(<version>)` suffix**. The
240	version-of-record — where it lives and its bump cadence, see
241	[versioning.md](versioning.md) — is useful for confirming
242	you're running the version you're testing, not a per-commit
243	title marker. We think per-commit version-in-title is
244	unstable on large projects: many changes are in flight
245	simultaneously, and a
246	version is only stable once merged into the main repo — so
247	titles don't carry one.
248	
249	### Title
250	
251	- ≤50 chars total.
252	- Common types: `feat`, `fix`, `refactor`, `test`,
253	  `docs`, `chore`.
254	- Favor terse phrasings.
255	- **Distinct per step** — each of a cycle's commits gets its
256	  own descriptive title (no shared cycle title with a step
257	  marker). Share a greppable stem across the cycle's titles
258	  (e.g. `ring buffer`) so `git log --grep` collects them; the
259	  chores section header matches the close-out title. See
260	  AGENTS.md
261	  [Conventional-commit shape](../AGENTS.md#conventional-commit-shape-ladder--chores--commit).
262	
263	### Body
264	
265	[Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form) (intro + bullets),
266	wrap ≤72. Bullet content differs per repo:
267	
268	- **App-repo body**: file-by-file. One bullet per file
269	  changed (file plus a one-line gist). Sub-bullets for
270	  files with multiple distinct changes:
271	
272	  ```
273	  - path/to/file1
274	    - first distinct change
275	    - second distinct change, wrapping to next line
276	       with continuation indented 5
277	  ```
278	
279	  The file-by-file list is the source of truth for the
280	  cycle's mechanical change record. Chores carries the
281	  narrative + design, not a copy of it. Promote any
282	  "why" beyond one sentence to a chores `###` subsection.
283	
284	- **Session-repo (`.claude`) body**: bullets describe
285	  in-session activity rather than code changes.
286	
287	### Trailer
288	
289	`ochid:` as the last line of the body — see
290	[Cross-repo linking (ochid trailers)](../AGENTS.md#cross-repo-linking-ochid-trailers)
291	in AGENTS.md for the convention.
292	
293	For breaking changes, use the hyphenated `BREAKING-CHANGE:`
294	trailer key. `BREAKING CHANGE:` (with a space) is the only
295	space-separated key the Conventional Commits spec allows; the
296	hyphenated form is also valid and avoids the space ambiguity.
297	
298	## Reviewing changes
299	
300	Work review looks at the **uncommitted working-copy diff**,
301	on the way to commit. The user opens diffs in their
302	editor (Zed, VSCode); jj commands are for terminal:
303	
304	- `jj diff` — working-copy diff (uncommitted)
305	- `jj diff -r @-` — diff of the previous commit
306	- `jj diff --from <X> --to <Y>` — any two revisions
307	- `jj show -r <X>` — description + diff for one rev
308	
309	Don't `jj edit -r @-` to view a past commit — that marks
310	it mutable and shifts `@`; use `jj diff -r @-` or
311	`jj show -r @-`.
312	
313	See [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders) for the
314	close-out squash recipe and recovery; revset primitives
315	are in [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets).
316	
317	## Pushing
318	
319	### Policy
320	
321	Push is **discretionary** during the cycle (backup,
322	progress visibility) and **mandatory at close-out** —
323	the cycle's result must be published.
324	
325	**Approval is per-push.** Every push — any repo, any kind:
326	cycle push, interim backup, recovery/surgery force-push —
327	happens only after the user has reviewed the changes to be
328	published and explicitly approved that specific push.
329	Approval of a plan that *includes* a push does not authorize
330	the push itself; stop and ask again at the moment of pushing.
331	
332	**Default is interactive; an explicit scoped delegation waives
333	the gates.** The gates above — per-push approval, the
334	commit-description review (show title+body and stop), and the
335	hard stop after push/finalize — are the *interactive default*.
336	They yield when the user **explicitly** delegates a complete,
337	bounded task and authorizes carrying it through ("do all of X
338	and push each step, don't check in"). The bot then proceeds
339	through that task's commits and pushes without stopping, and
340	continues past each push to the next step. Conditions:
341	
342	- **Explicit grant** — never inferred from a task merely being
343	  well-scoped; the user's words must authorize unattended
344	  completion. "Commit and push" (or "then push") names the
345	  destination, not a waiver: it authorizes the push *after*
346	  the normal work review and description review, not skipping
347	  them. Only wording that explicitly waives the stops ("don't
348	  check in", "no need to review", "carry it through
349	  unattended") waives them.
350	- **Bounded goal** — covers the named task only; does not carry
351	  to the next task or a vaguer follow-on.
352	- **Destructive ops still pause** — delegation covers the task's
353	  ordinary commits and pushes; it does *not* pre-authorize a
354	  genuinely irreversible action (force-push over published
355	  history, history rewrite, deleting a remote branch). Those can
356	  permanently destroy work and aren't a normal cycle step, so the
357	  bot flags one before acting. An ordinary delegated cycle never
358	  reaches this.
359	- **Still transparent** — report each commit/push as it lands
360	  (title + outcome) so the user can catch up.
361	- **When in doubt, ask** — ambiguous authorization falls back to
362	  per-push approval.
363	
364	### Shape at close-out push
365	
366	At close-out the cycle's *work* is done; its *published
367	shape* is the remaining choice, made at push time. Surface
368	the options and get user approval before pushing — once on
369	the target, changing shape is a remote rewrite (force-push,
370	needs approval), so choose deliberately.
371	
372	- **Squash to one commit** — single entry on the target.
373	  Right for straightforward changes where the Work-N is
374	  focused on one or two files.
375	- **Merge non-ff** *(current default)* — `main` gains a
376	  merge commit (`X.Y.Z`); cycle commits stay reachable via
377	  two parents. `jj log -r ..@ -n <N>` shows the trapezoidal
378	  shape. See [Merge non-ff recipe](#merge-non-ff-recipe)
379	  for the full setup sequence.
380	- **Keep separate** — one commit per cycle entry on
381	  `main`. Use when the decomposition itself is
382	  informative. Each chores section keeps its own header /
383	  `Commits:` ref; no consolidation churn.
384	
385	Set up squash/merge before invoking `vc-x1 push`; use
386	`jj git push` directly for non-standard shapes.
387	
388	### Merge non-ff recipe
389	
390	Setting up a [Merge non-ff](#shape-at-close-out-push)
391	close-out is a fixed sequence. `<closeout>` is the cycle's
392	close-out commit, `<prev>` the previous cycle's close-out
393	(the current `main` tip), `<work-tip>` the cycle's last
394	Work commit:
395	
396	1. **Rebase the close-out into a merge** — `jj rebase -r
397	   <closeout> --onto <prev> --onto <work-tip>`.
398	   - `-r <closeout>` keeps the `<closeout>` commit in place.
399	   - `--onto <prev>` becomes its first parent (trunk).
400	   - `--onto <work-tip>` becomes the second parent.
401	
402	   Together these make `<closeout>` a merge of
403	   `<prev>` + `<work-tip>`, forming a trapezoidal commit.
404	2. **Use `jj new <merge>`** to add an empty `@` above the
405	   merge. The rebase left `@` *on* the now-content-bearing
406	   merge, which git/IDE diff views show as uncommitted;
407	   `jj new` restores the clean empty `@` on top.
408	3. **Push** — `jj git push --bookmark main -R .`.
409	
410	**Post-hoc caveat.** If the cycle was already pushed
411	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) its commits are
412	immutable: the rebase needs `--ignore-immutable` and the
413	push force-updates `main`.
414	The standard sequence assumes the merge is set up *before*
415	the close-out push.
416	
417	### vc-x1 push wrapper
418	
419	`vc-x1 push <bookmark>` wraps per-push mechanics. See
420	`vc-x1 push --help` for current flags.
421	
422	**Current limitation**: only fully supports the
423	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) shape; other
424	shapes need manual jj steps. Improvements tracked /
425	planned:
426	
427	- N:1 code↔bot for Merge non-ff (`## Todo` entry P1).
428	- Symmetric squash (planned, to be captured in `-2`).
429	- Per-repo bookmark names (planned).
430	
431	### .claude cadence
432	
433	**Cadence**: one push = one `.claude` commit, paired
434	with every code commit in that push.
435	
436	The `.claude` working copy accumulates session data
437	across the cycle; its change ID stays stable across
438	snapshots, `jj describe`, and the finalize commit, so
439	code-side `ochid:` trailers resolve.
440	
441	`.claude` is a linear journal — all session work lives
442	on `main`, regardless of the app-side bookmark. **Do not
443	create or maintain `.claude` bookmarks that mirror
444	app-side branches** — risks the bot steering session
445	pushes to the wrong remote ref.
446	
447	Ending a session: if the user runs `/exit` there will be
448	session information created, which we don't worry about.
449	The user can close the terminal instead and `@` will
450	remain empty.
451	
452	### Bot communication at the reviews
453	
454	Use plain prose — no insider jargon ("Gate N signal",
455	"Checkpoint N", etc.):
456	
457	- **At Work review** — summarize what changed and stop.
458	  "Work complete. Please review."
459	- **At Commit Description review** — present `$TITLE`
460	  and `$BODY` explicitly; ask permission to commit/push.
461	  Don't spell out the full `vc-x1 push ... --title ...
462	  --body ...` invocation by default.
463	- **At Post close-out review** — surface the shape
464	  options (squash / merge / keep) and the push target;
465	  wait for the user's choice before any `jj squash` /
466	  `jj rebase` / `jj git push` invocation.
467	
468	### After push or finalize: stop and wait
469	
470	After you **push** (cross the remote boundary) or launch
471	**finalize** on the `.claude` repo (`vc-x1 finalize --repo
472	.claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach`) — by
473	hand or via the `vc-x1 push` wrapper — you **MUST NEVER**
474	proceed (next step, edit, tool call, text output) until the
475	user explicitly directs you to continue. **Even when the next
476	step seems obvious — wait.** Treat push-or-finalize as a hard
477	stop for the whole turn.
478	
479	`vc-x1 push` performs both as its tail stages — `push-app`
480	then the detached `vc-x1 finalize` on `.claude` — so there is
481	no gap left to speak in once the push is invoked. Put **all**
482	closing words *before* invoking it. The harness rejects an empty
483	turn, so it may force a visible token after the tool returns; if
484	so, emit a bare acknowledgment only (e.g. "landed") — never a
485	summary, verification, or next-step offer.
486	
487	Why (`.claude` is the live journal finalize snapshots →
488	squashes → pushes):
489	
490	- **`--detach`**: lets the tool return immediately so the
491	  harness can keep flushing the *trailing* session-data — the
492	  tool call's own transcript and any words emitted *before*
493	  the invoke — into the snapshot.
494	- **`--delay 10`**: gives the harness time to complete that
495	  flush before the snapshot is taken. It is **not** time for
496	  the bot to do more work.
497	- **silence**: the bot must not summarize or perform any work
498	  after the tool returns. The harness may force a visible token
499	  (it rejects an empty turn) — keep it to a bare acknowledgment,
500	  never a summary or a next step.
501	
502	**Known slip**: the bot has emitted a summary after launching
503	finalize. There is no "harmless" closing line — if it's worth
504	saying, say it *before* the invoke.
505	
506	### Recovery
507	
508	- **If push exits before `finalize-claude`** (e.g.
509	  failure between `push-app` and `finalize-claude`), run
510	  finalize by hand:
511	
512	  ```
513	  vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach --log /tmp/vc-x1-finalize.log
514	  ```
515	
516	- **Run finalize again if `@` is non-empty** after the
517	  finalize's squash-and-commit (also desirable after
518	  extra activity by the bot's agents).
519	  - Why: finalize snapshots `.claude` at `--delay`, but the
520	    bot keeps writing session data while it runs — the tool's
521	    own invocation plus the bot's closing response land
522	    *after* the snapshot.
523	  - Safe to repeat: bot session data is append-only, so a
524	    re-run never conflicts or overwrites. (This could
525	    change; it is not under the user's control.)
526	  - Fix: run `vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push
527	    <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach` another time to capture
528	    that tail; one extra pass is generally enough.
529	  - No guarantees: events outside the bot's control can leave
530	    `@` non-empty — e.g. the bot's back end may decide to
531	    squash/consolidate session data, which can take minutes
532	    and land after the snapshot. The remedy is the same: just
533	    run finalize again. This is why a single pass is never
534	    guaranteed to leave `@` empty.
535	- **Clear push's saved state** after any out-of-band
536	  recovery — `rm .vc-x1/push-state.toml` or `vc-x1 push
537	  <bookmark> --restart` — otherwise push resumes from a
538	  stale stage.
539	- **Late code-repo tweak after `push-app` succeeded**
540	  (e.g. updating AGENTS.md or memory) requires `jj
541	  squash --ignore-immutable` and a re-push; that is a
542	  remote rewrite and needs explicit approval like any
543	  push.
544	
545	## Iterative work
546	
547	When work for a single commit (the **target**) benefits
548	from incremental review, loop:
549	
550	1. `jj new -R .` — fresh empty `@` on top of the target.
551	2. Make the next round of changes.
552	3. User reviews the round (see
553	   [Reviewing changes](#reviewing-changes)).
554	4. `jj squash -R .` folds into the target and creates a
555	   new empty `@`.
556	5. If not done, go to step 2.
557	
558	Same jj mechanics as a
559	[sub-cycle ladder](#sub-cycle-ladders), but at
560	single-commit scope — the version
561	doesn't change.
562	
563	## Sub-cycle ladders
564	
565	When a Work commit subdivides into a sub-cycle (see
566	[Numbering](#numbering) for suffix nesting), its Work
567	commits typically live as a local jj `@` chain and
568	**collapse into the sub-cycle's Close-out** before the
569	parent cycle continues. Ladder commits are scratch —
570	for review and bisection only.
571	
572	### Per-Work-commit contract within a ladder
573	
574	For each Work commit in the ladder:
575	
576	1. `jj new -R .` — create a fresh empty `@`.
577	2. Do the commit's work.
578	3. Run the fast validation (Rust example: `cargo test
579	   --bins`). **Non-negotiable** — for code, build and clippy
580	   alone miss regressions until a later commit runs the full
581	   suite, raising bisection cost.
582	4. `jj describe -m "..." -m "..." -R .` — working title
583	   only; the sub-cycle Close-out collects everything
584	   into one final commit.
585	
586	### Navigating the ladder
587	
588	Common moves:
589	
590	- `jj log -r '<base>::' -R .` — see the whole ladder
591	  from its base.
592	- `jj edit -r <prefix> -R .` — jump `@` to any ladder
593	  commit by chid prefix; useful for bisection.
594	- `jj edit @-- -R .` — quick-jump back two commits.
595	- `jj diff -r <chid> -R .` — review one commit in
596	  isolation.
597	
598	Modifications to any ladder commit rewrite it in place;
599	descendants auto-rebase.
600	
601	### Close-out: squash the ladder
602	
603	When all ladder Work commits are done and tests pass:
604	
605	```
606	jj squash --from "<base>..@-" --into @ -u -R .
607	```
608	
609	`<base>` is the parent of the first ladder commit; `-u`
610	keeps `@`'s description and discards the sources'.
611	After squash, history is linear: `<base> → @`;
612	intermediate commits are auto-abandoned.
613	
614	Then `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` as for any other commit.
615	
616	For N = 1 the squash is a no-op (`<base>..@-` is empty
617	when `@-` is `<base>`); push the single commit directly.
618	
619	### Recovery
620	
621	If a ladder commit goes wrong, back out without losing
622	prior commits:
623	
624	- **Discard the current commit.** `jj abandon @ -R .`
625	  drops it; you get a fresh empty `@` on the same
626	  parent.
627	- **Edit an earlier commit.** `jj edit -r <chid> -R .`,
628	  make corrections, then `jj edit -r <last-ladder-chid>`
629	  to return. Descendants auto-rebase.
630	- **Discard the entire ladder.** `jj op log -R .` shows
631	  the op history; `jj op restore <op-id> -R .` reverts
632	  to that point. Full undo — removes *all* ladder work
633	  after the chosen op. Use only to start over.
634	
635	# References
636	
637	- [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets) — revset primitives
638	  (chid/cid, `@`/`@-`/`@+`, `..`/`::` ranges, prefix matching).
639	- [`substep-test.sh`](substep-test.sh) — script that
640	  scaffolds a 4-revision ladder under `/tmp/substep-test`
641	  for squash-recipe experiments.
642	- The per-commit `cargo test --bins` gate exists because a
643	  regression introduced in an early ladder commit can go
644	  uncaught until a later commit runs the full suite, raising
645	  bisection cost.
646	
```

1	# Versioning
2	
3	How this project versions its commits and the running
4	artifact. The scheme — the cycle step-numbering and the
5	unique-per-commit aim — is generic and shared across projects:
6	this file is copied **verbatim**, with
7	[Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
8	covering each medium by conditional rather than per-project
9	edits.
10	
11	## Terms
12	
13	Three names, used as defined here across
14	[AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md),
15	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md), and the notes files:
16	
17	- **version** — the per-commit version (e.g. `0.3.0-5.3.0`),
18	  written with `-` in prose, ladders, chores, todo, and commit
19	  talk; its suffix encodes the cycle phase (see
20	  [Step numbering](#step-numbering)).
21	- **version-of-record** — the authoritative stored copy of the
22	  version, in the project's manifest (see
23	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record));
24	  a built or running artifact derives from it.
25	- **versioning** — the topic: this scheme as a whole.
26	
27	## Recording the version-of-record
28	
29	Where the version-of-record lives, how it's stored and
30	surfaced, and how often it changes — pick the case that fits
31	your medium:
32	
33	- **Manifest** — where the version-of-record is stored:
34	  - if Rust, `Cargo.toml` `[package].version`
35	  - if Python, `pyproject.toml` `[project].version` (or the
36	    committed config it's sourced from)
37	  - otherwise wherever the medium records it (a generic
38	    `version.toml`, a book's frontmatter, …) — add the case as
39	    needed
40	- **Notation** — how the `-` form is stored:
41	  - if the format allows `-` (TOML `version.toml`, `Cargo.toml`),
42	    store it verbatim
43	  - if it bars `-` (PEP 440's local segment, e.g. a Python
44	    project), remap to `+` — `0.3.0-5.3.0` → `0.3.0+5.3.0`: same
45	    version, just the stored spelling
46	- **Reporter** — how a built artifact surfaces the
47	  version-of-record:
48	  - if a CLI app, `<cli-app> -V`
49	  - if a TUI/GUI, add to Help/About or display on the title
50	- **Cadence** — how often to bump: see
51	  [Unique per commit](#unique-per-commit-preference-not-requirement);
52	  this project follows the per-commit preference.
53	
54	## Unique per commit (preference, not requirement)
55	
56	Our general notion is that the version-of-record should change
57	on **every commit**, so a built or running artifact identifies
58	the exact commit it came from.
59	
60	- This is a preference, **not** a hard requirement. A project
61	  following the cycle protocol may bump less often — once per
62	  cycle, only at release, and so on. Record the choice in
63	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
64	  if it differs.
65	- It is achievable because the cycle's versions (below) are
66	  **pre-assignable**, unlike the git SHA, which a commit cannot
67	  contain (see the cycle protocol's
68	  [Commits backfill](cycle-protocol.md#commits-backfill)).
69	
70	## Step numbering
71	
72	The cycle (Preparation -> Work -> Close-out; see
73	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md)) encodes each commit's
74	phase in the version suffix — the **final identifier `0` marks
75	a Preparation**:
76	
77	- `X.Y.Z-0` — Preparation
78	- `X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, … — Work commits
79	- `X.Y.Z` — Close-out (bare version, no suffix)
80	
81	**Preparation is optional.** A lightweight cycle — no ladder,
82	no setup commit — skips `-0` and starts at `-1` (its first
83	Work commit). The same holds at every level: a sub-cycle
84	needing no Preparation omits its `.0` (see Nesting). One that
85	grows a Preparation later adds the `0` step without
86	renumbering siblings.
87	
88	Disambiguation:
89	
90	- `-10` — Work commit #10 (final identifier `10`), not a
91	  Preparation.
92	- `-1.0` — Preparation of the `-1` sub-cycle (final
93	  identifier `0`).
94	
95	**Nesting.** Sub-cycles append another level, recursively:
96	
97	- `X.Y.Z-3.0` — Preparation of the `-3` sub-cycle
98	- `X.Y.Z-3.1`, `X.Y.Z-3.2` — its Work
99	- `X.Y.Z-3` — its Close-out
100	- `X.Y.Z-3.1.0` — Preparation of the `-3.1` sub-sub-cycle
101	
102	Bump the version-of-record at the start of each phase, so the
103	active phase is recorded — and, per the preference above,
104	every commit carries a distinct version.
105	
```

> AGENT

What would you like to clarify about the harness-fix shape or the cycle shape?

> DEVELOPER

Saturating + suspend detect separate, yes. But what is what is magical about runs > 60s?

> AGENT

Nothing magical at all — 60 s is just the number we typed. `new_hist()` reads:

```rust
// 1 ns to 60 s, 3 sig figs
Histogram::<u64>::new_with_bounds(1, 60_000_000_000, 3).unwrap()
```

hdrhistogram with resize disabled requires you to declare a `[low, high]` range up front, so *some* bound had to be picked, and 60 s was chosen as "comfortably beyond any legitimate single sample in a nanosecond-scale microbenchmark" — a sane-world ceiling, not a technical limit. The tradeoff the bound controls is memory, and it's mild: at 3 significant figures the histogram uses ~1024 counters per power-of-two of range, so 1 ns–60 s costs about 220 KB, and stretching to the full u64-ish range (~292 years) only doubles that to ~440 KB. We could raise it to an hour or to `u64::MAX/2` essentially for free.

The reason I'd *keep* 60 s rather than raise it: any single sample that even approaches it means the run is already broken — either the machine slept or something wedged — so recording the "true" 3.5-hour value adds no information, it just distorts mean/stdev by a larger amount. Clamp at the sane-world ceiling, and let the suspend detector say *why* the run is invalid. With `saturating_record`, values above the bound record as 60 s exactly, so an unexplained pileup at 60 s is itself visible in the max column even if the detector somehow missed the cause.

One clarification on your "separate, yes" before I start: did you mean

1. the original plan — two commits total: one harness commit (saturating **and** suspend-detect together) + one docs commit, or
2. three commits — saturating, suspend-detect, and the docs note each landing as its own single-commit change?

Both parts of the harness fix are independent enough to split cleanly, so either works; 2 is the stricter reading of "unrelated changes land separately."

> DEVELOPER

"separate, yes", was the original plan. Any documenation should be with the change. So the 60 s is related to the length of a sample not a run?

> AGENT

Yes — per **sample**, not per run. Each histogram entry is one outer-loop iteration: `elapsed_of_inner_loop / inner`, i.e. an averaged per-call latency for that sample (`src/harness.rs:147-158`). A 600 s run records ~3 billion such values, each expected in the 4–130 ns range; the 60 s bound is checked against each individual value as it's recorded, and run length is irrelevant to it. That's also why longer runs *look* like the trigger: more wall-clock exposed means more chance a suspend lands inside one sample and inflates that single value past 60 s.

Plan confirmed: two one-commit changes, docs riding with their change. Starting commit 1 (harness: `saturating_record` + suspend detection). Let me read what I'll touch.

1	# Chores 04
2	
3	Continuation of [chores-03](../chores-03.md). Records landed work;
4	conventions in [AGENTS.md](../../AGENTS.md#chores-conventions) and
5	[cycle-protocol.md](../cycle-protocol.md#chores-sections).
6	
7	## feat: zcr bench family (raw/with/spin, 1t/2t)
8	
9	Commits: [[1]],[[2]],[[3]],[[4]],[[5]]
10	
11	`../zc-ring-x1` exposes three API tiers per endpoint — raw
12	`reserve_slot` (caller handles Full/Empty), `reserve_slot_with`
13	(injected wait-policy closure), and `reserve_slot_spin` (built-in
14	spin policy). Its docs claim the no-wait fast path does the same
15	loads at every tier; add six benches `zcr-{raw,with,spin}-{1t,2t}`
16	to verify the tiers perform basically the same.
17	
18	### As-built ladder
19	
20	- 0.13.0-0 `chore: adopt cycle protocol + open zcr cycle`
21	- 0.13.0-1 `feat: zcr-raw-1t/2t ring benches`
22	- 0.13.0-2 `feat: zcr-with-1t/2t ring benches`
23	- 0.13.0-3 `feat: zcr-spin-1t/2t ring benches`
24	- 0.13.0-4 `docs: zcr tier comparison results`
25	- 0.13.0 `feat: zcr bench family (raw/with/spin, 1t/2t)` —
26	  close-out
27	
28	The cycle was planned as 0.12.0 but renumbered at `-0`: the
29	session started from a working copy based on 0.11.0, unaware
30	the aarch64 ticks cycle (0.12.0, pushed from another machine)
31	already held `main`. The `-0` commit was rebased onto it —
32	`jj new main` → `vc-x1 sync` (no-op, already fetched) →
33	`jj rebase -r <prep> -d main` → conflict resolution (Cargo
34	version line; union-merge of `.claude/settings.local.json`) —
35	and the cycle renumbered 0.12.0 → 0.13.0.
36	
37	### Tier comparison (0.13.0-4)
38	
39	Method: installed `iiac-perf 0.13.0-4`, Ryzen 9 3900X, idle
40	desktop, `iiac-perf zcr mpsc-2t-spin ice-ps-2t -d 5 --pin 0,1`;
41	values are the adjusted trimmed mean (min-p99). Run 2 repeats
42	the 2t trio to check repeatability.
43	
44	| bench        | run 1  | run 2  |
45	|--------------|-------:|-------:|
46	| zcr-raw-1t   |   4 ns |      — |
47	| zcr-with-1t  |   4 ns |      — |
48	| zcr-spin-1t  |   5 ns |      — |
49	| zcr-raw-2t   | 119 ns | 120 ns |
50	| zcr-with-2t  | 101 ns | 101 ns |
51	| zcr-spin-2t  | 101 ns |  99 ns |
52	| mpsc-2t-spin | 117 ns |      — |
53	| ice-ps-2t    | 656 ns |      — |
54	
55	Observations:
56	
57	- 1t: all three tiers are identical within noise (4-5 ns).
58	  The no-wait fast path costs the same at every tier, matching
59	  the zc-ring-x1 doc claim that `_with`'s fast path does
60	  exactly the loads `reserve_slot` does.
61	- 2t: `with` and `spin` are identical (~100 ns) — expected,
62	  `reserve_slot_spin` *is* `reserve_slot_with(policy::spin)`.
63	  `raw` is consistently ~20 ns slower (119-120 ns) across
64	  runs.
65	- We think the raw 2t penalty is in the waiting path, not the
66	  fast path: the hand-written retry loop re-enters
67	  `reserve_slot` on every failed attempt — re-running the type
68	  check and re-loading the caller's own index each time — while
69	  `_with` hoists the own-index load out of the loop and re-reads
70	  only the peer index per attempt, so it reacts faster when the
71	  peer's index store lands.
72	- Transport context: the zcr 2t rows (~100-120 ns) sit at or
73	  under `mpsc-2t-spin` (117 ns) and ~6x under `ice-ps-2t`
74	  (656 ns) at the same wait policy.
75	
76	Net: the ergonomic tiers cost nothing over raw — and under
77	cross-core traffic the DIY retry loop is the *slowest* way to
78	wait.
79

354	        let mut trim_var_sum = 0.0f64;
355	        let mut trim_var_count = 0u64;
356	        let mut cum = 0u64;
357	        for iv in hist.iter_recorded() {
358	            let value = iv.value_iterated_to();
359	            let count = iv.count_at_value();
360	            let mid_rank = (cum as f64 + count as f64 / 2.0) / sample_count as f64;
361	            let idx = boundary_pcts[1..]
362	                .iter()
363	                .position(|&b| mid_rank < b)
364	                .unwrap_or(n_bands - 1);
365	            if idx < n_bands - 1 {
366	                let diff = value as f64 - trim_mean;
367	                trim_var_sum += diff * diff * count as f64;
368	                trim_var_count += count;
369	            }
370	            cum += count;
371	        }
372	        let trim_stdev = if trim_var_count > 1 {
373	            (trim_var_sum / trim_var_count as f64).sqrt()
374	        } else {
375	            0.0
376	        };
377	
378	        println!(
379	            "{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>skip$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns{GAP}{:>adj_w$} ns",
380	            "mean min-p99",
381	            "",
382	            fmt_commas_f64(trim_mean, 0),
383	            fmt_commas_f64(trim_adj, 0),
384	        );
385	        println!(
386	            "{INDENT}{:<label_w$} {:>skip$}{GAP}{:>mean_w$} ns",
387	            "stdev min-p99",
388	            "",
389	            fmt_commas_f64(trim_stdev, 0),
390	        );
391	    }
392	    println!();
393	}
394	
```

```
51	## Usage
52	
53	```
54	iiac-perf [BENCH...] [-d SECONDS] [-o OUTER] [-i INNER]
55	```
56	
57	`BENCH` is one or more registered bench names, or `all` for every
58	registered bench. A name that matches no bench exactly runs every
59	bench it is a prefix of — `ice` runs all iceoryx2 benches, `mpsc`
60	runs `mpsc-1t` and `mpsc-2t`. **With no arguments, `iiac-perf` prints the
61	available list and exits — that's the source of truth for which
62	benches the current build registers.**
63	
64	Flags (also visible via `-h` / `--help`):
65	- `-d`, `--duration SECONDS` — target wall-clock seconds per bench
66	  (default `5.0`); the outer loop runs until this time is reached
67	  (inner auto-sizes). See chores `0.3.1-dev1` for the empirical
68	  study behind the default — longer (`-d 30`+) gives
69	  publication-grade stability. Mutually exclusive with `-D`.
70	- `-D`, `--total-duration SECONDS` — target total wall-clock seconds
71	  across all requested benches; budget is split equally per bench
72	  (e.g. `-D 30` with 6 benches → 5 s each). Mutually exclusive with
73	  `-d`.
74	- `-o`, `--outer N` — override outer loop count (forces count-based
75	  mode instead of time-based; inner still adapts).
76	- `-i`, `--inner N` — override inner loop count per histogram sample.
77	  `inner=1` measures single-call latency (each sample = one step).
78	  Higher inner measures back-to-back / burst rate (each sample = N
79	  steps averaged, hides per-call jitter and parking costs).
80	- `--pin CORES` — pin bench threads to logical CPUs. `CORES` is a
81	  comma-separated list with optional ranges: `0,1`, `0-5`, `0,3-5,7`.
82	  Treated as a **core pool** indexed positionally with wrap-around, so
83	  thread `i` gets `pool[i % pool.len()]`. Examples:
84	  - `--pin 0,1` pins a 2-thread bench to logical CPUs 0 and 1.
85	  - `--pin 0,0` co-locates two threads on the same CPU
86	    (oversubscription — measures contention).
87	  - `--pin 0-11` defines a 12-CPU pool for larger fanout benches;
88	    threads wrap over it.
89	
90	  On AMD Zen 2 (e.g. Ryzen 9 3900X, 12 physical × 2 SMT = 24 logical
91	  CPUs), logical IDs `N` and `N+12` are SMT siblings of the same
92	  physical core. `--pin 0,12` pairs siblings (max resource contention);
93	  `--pin 0,1` uses independent physical cores in the same CCX (best
94	  latency for channel benches — shared L3, no SMT contention). Check
95	  your topology with
96	  `cat /sys/devices/system/cpu/cpu0/topology/thread_siblings_list`.
97	
98	  Typical measured effect on `mpsc-2t` at `-d 10` (3900X, idle desktop):
99	  unpinned mean ≈ 7,044 ns / stdev ≈ 6,545 ns / p99.99 ≈ 74 µs;
100	  `--pin 0,1` → mean ≈ 5,636 ns / stdev ≈ 1,321 ns / p99.99 ≈ 17 µs.
101	  Tail tightens ~4×, stdev ~5×, mean drops ~20 %.
102	- `--no-pin-cal` — skip pinning the main thread for calibration.
103	  By default, calibration runs with main pinned to `pin[0]` (if
104	  `--pin` is set) or core 0, so framing/loop numbers come from a
105	  stable environment regardless of `--pin`. Pass this flag to
106	  reproduce pre-0.6.0 behavior (main pinned iff `--pin` is given)
107	  for A/B comparison. No effect when `--pin` is set.
108	- `-v`, `--verbose` — print internals to stderr (affinity mask at
109	  startup, calibration parameters, raw `min_low`/`min_high`,
110	  precise fit values, calibration wall time). Equivalent to
111	  `RUST_LOG=debug`. Default filter is `warn` — silent unless
112	  something is wrong. `RUST_LOG`, when set, wins over `-v` so
113	  per-module filtering still works.
114	- `-t`, `--ticks` — show `TProbe` results in raw hardware tick
115	  counts (`tk`; x86_64 TSC, aarch64 `CNTVCT_EL0`) instead of
116	  converting to nanoseconds. Only affects `TProbe`-based benches
117	  (e.g. `tp-pc`); `Probe`-based output is always in nanoseconds.
118	  Use this to inspect the underlying tick counts directly, e.g.
119	  when comparing against the counter frequency.
120	
121	Each bench prints a band-based histogram in nanoseconds. Bands are
122	defined by percentile boundaries (min→p1, p1→p10, …, p99→max) and
123	show first, last, range (`last - first + 1`), count, mean, and
124	adjusted mean. The adjusted column subtracts apparatus overhead
125	(`framing_per_sample / inner + loop_per_iter`), calibrated once at
126	startup via a two-point fit on an empty bench. The startup banner
127	reports `cal pin` (calibration pinning) and `bench pin` (per-bench
128	thread pool) separately.
129	
130	Examples:
131	
132	```
133	iiac-perf                                # list available benches
134	iiac-perf all                            # every bench, default ~5s each
135	iiac-perf min-now -d 30                  # one bench, 30s budget
136	iiac-perf all -D 30                      # ~30s total split equally
137	iiac-perf mpsc-2t -i 1                   # explicit single-call latency
138	iiac-perf mpsc-2t -i 100                 # back-to-back rate
139	iiac-perf mpsc-2t --pin 0,1              # pinned, different physical cores
140	iiac-perf mpsc-2t --pin 0,12             # pinned, SMT siblings (contention)
141	iiac-perf mpsc-2t -v                     # show cal internals (affinity, raw fit)
142	iiac-perf mpsc-2t --no-pin-cal           # skip the cal-time pin (bench unchanged)
143	iiac-perf mpsc-2t --pin 5,10 --no-pin-cal # bench on 5,10; cal on full mask
144	RUST_LOG=info iiac-perf mpsc-2t          # info-level only (overrides -v)
145	```
146	
147	## Example runs
148	
149	All measurements below are on a Ryzen 9 3900X, idle desktop,
150	`mpsc-2t -d 3`. Numbers vary run-to-run and machine-to-machine;
```

The high\n/// bound is a sane-world ceiling for one recorded sample, not a\n/// technical limit — [`record_sample`] clamps above it and\n/// [`warn_invalid`] flags the run.\nconst HIST_LOW_NS: u64 = 1;\nconst HIST_HIGH_NS: u64 = 60_000_000_000;\n\n/// `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` minus `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` elapsed divergence\n/// (seconds) at or above which [`warn_invalid`] reports that the\n/// system suspended during the run.\nconst SUSPEND_WARN_S: f64 = 1.0;"

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/src/harness.rs",
  "old_string": "fn new_hist() -> Histogram<u64> {\n    // 1 ns to 60 s, 3 sig figs\n    Histogram::<u64>::new_with_bounds(1, 60_000_000_000, 3).unwrap()\n}\n\nfn record_sample<B: Bench>(bench: &mut B, inner: u64, hist: &mut Histogram<u64>) {\n    let start = minstant::Instant::now();\n    for _ in 0..inner {\n        black_box(bench.step());\n    }\n    let elapsed_ns = start.elapsed().as_nanos() as u64;\n    hist.record(round_elapsed(elapsed_ns, inner)).unwrap();\n}",
  "new_string": "/// Fresh histogram over `[HIST_LOW_NS, HIST_HIGH_NS]` at 3 sig\n/// figs, resize disabled — out-of-range samples clamp (see\n/// [`record_sample`]) rather than grow the histogram.\nfn new_hist() -> Histogram<u64> {\n    Histogram::<u64>::new_with_bounds(HIST_LOW_NS, HIST_HIGH_NS, 3).unwrap() // OK: constant bounds\n}\n\n/// Time one sample (`inner` back-to-back calls), divide down to a\n/// per-call value, and record it, clamping at the histogram\n/// bounds — a suspend-inflated or wedged sample must not panic a\n/// long run ([`warn_invalid`] flags it instead).\nfn record_sample<B: Bench>(bench: &mut B, inner: u64, hist: &mut Histogram<u64>) {\n    let start = minstant::Instant::now();\n    for _ in 0..inner {\n        black_box(bench.step());\n    }\n    let elapsed_ns = start.elapsed().as_nanos() as u64;\n    hist.saturating_record(round_elapsed(elapsed_ns, inner));\n}"
}

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/src/harness.rs",
  "old_string": "fn round_elapsed(elapsed_ns: u64, inner: u64) -> u64 {\n    (elapsed_ns + inner / 2) / inner\n}",
  "new_string": "fn round_elapsed(elapsed_ns: u64, inner: u64) -> u64 {\n    (elapsed_ns + inner / 2) / inner\n}\n\n/// Paired run-start readings of `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` and\n/// `CLOCK_BOOTTIME`, for detecting a system suspend that spanned\n/// a measurement run.\n///\n/// - `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` freezes while the system is suspended;\n///   `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` keeps counting — the divergence of the two\n///   elapsed times is the time spent suspended.\n/// - Uses std `Instant` (`CLOCK_MONOTONIC`), not `minstant`: we\n///   think the TSC keeps counting across s2idle suspend, which is\n///   exactly the clock behavior being detected.\nstruct ClockPair {\n    mono: std::time::Instant,\n    boot_ns: u64,\n}\n\nimpl ClockPair {\n    /// Capture both clocks now.\n    fn now() -> Self {\n        Self {\n            mono: std::time::Instant::now(),\n            boot_ns: boottime_ns(),\n        }\n    }\n\n    /// Seconds the system spent suspended since [`now`][Self::now]:\n    /// boottime elapsed minus monotonic elapsed (~0 when no\n    /// suspend occurred).\n    fn suspended_s(&self) -> f64 {\n        let boot_s = (boottime_ns() - self.boot_ns) as f64 / 1e9;\n        let mono_s = self.mono.elapsed().as_nanos() as f64 / 1e9;\n        boot_s - mono_s\n    }\n}\n\n/// Current `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` reading in nanoseconds.\nfn boottime_ns() -> u64 {\n    let mut ts = libc::timespec {\n        tv_sec: 0,\n        tv_nsec: 0,\n    };\n    // SAFETY: clock_gettime only writes `ts`; CLOCK_BOOTTIME is\n    // always valid on Linux.\n    let rc = unsafe { libc::clock_gettime(libc::CLOCK_BOOTTIME, &mut ts) };\n    assert_eq!(rc, 0, \"clock_gettime(CLOCK_BOOTTIME) failed\");\n    ts.tv_sec as u64 * 1_000_000_000 + ts.tv_nsec as u64\n}\n\n/// Print `WARNING` lines when the finished run's data is invalid:\n///\n/// - the system suspended during the run (clock divergence — a\n///   mid-sample suspend inflates that sample by the sleep gap,\n///   poisoning mean/stdev even under the histogram bound);\n/// - one or more samples clamped at [`HIST_HIGH_NS`] (a wedged or\n///   suspend-inflated sample with no detected suspend).\nfn warn_invalid(name: &str, hist: &Histogram<u64>, clocks: &ClockPair) {\n    let suspended_s = clocks.suspended_s();\n    if suspended_s >= SUSPEND_WARN_S {\n        println!(\"WARNING {name}: system suspended ~{suspended_s:.1}s during the run; data invalid\");\n    }\n    if hist.len() > 0 && hist.max() >= HIST_HIGH_NS {\n        println!(\n            \"WARNING {name}: sample(s) clamped at the {}s histogram bound; data invalid\",\n            HIST_HIGH_NS / 1_000_000_000\n        );\n    }\n}"
}
```

The startup banner\nreports `cal pin` (calibration pinning) and `bench pin` (per-bench\nthread pool) separately.\n\nA bench may print `WARNING` lines flagging its data as invalid:\n\n- **system suspended** — the run spanned a system suspend,\n  detected by `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` vs `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` elapsed\n  divergence. A mid-sample suspend inflates that one sample by\n  the whole sleep gap, poisoning mean/stdev (the trimmed\n  `min-p99` rows usually survive).\n- **sample(s) clamped** — a sample exceeded the histogram's 60 s\n  bound and was recorded as 60 s instead of aborting the run\n  (visible as a pileup at `max`)."
}

228	/// Print `WARNING` lines when the finished run's data is invalid:
229	///
230	/// - the system suspended during the run (clock divergence — a
231	///   mid-sample suspend inflates that sample by the sleep gap,
232	///   poisoning mean/stdev even under the histogram bound);
233	/// - one or more samples clamped at [`HIST_HIGH_NS`] (a wedged or
234	///   suspend-inflated sample with no detected suspend).
235	fn warn_invalid(name: &str, hist: &Histogram<u64>, clocks: &ClockPair) {
236	    let suspended_s = clocks.suspended_s();
237	    if suspended_s >= SUSPEND_WARN_S {
238	        println!(
239	            "WARNING {name}: system suspended ~{suspended_s:.1}s during the run; data invalid"
240	        );
241	    }
242	    if hist.len() > 0 && hist.max() >= HIST_HIGH_NS {
243	        println!(
244	            "WARNING {name}: sample(s) clamped at the {}s histogram bound; data invalid",
245	            HIST_HIGH_NS / 1_000_000_000
246	        );
247	    }
248	}

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/notes/chores/chores-04.md",
  "old_string": "# References\n\n[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/iiac-perf/commit/8aaccf8518c4 \"8aaccf8518c4cb46bcc2fbf96a317d5d4c962f68\"",
  "new_string": "## fix: saturate hist records, flag suspended runs\n\nCommits:\n\nTwo long zcr runs (`-d 600`, `-d 300`) panicked at\n`hist.record().unwrap()` with `ValueOutOfRangeResizeDisabled`:\nthe desktop idle-suspended mid-run, the in-flight sample spanned\nthe sleep, and at `inner=1` the raw gap exceeded the histogram's\n60 s high bound. Journal `PM: suspend exit` timestamps match both\npanics to the second. Sub-bound gaps are worse — they record\nsilently (`zcr-with-1t` logged a 40.6 s \"sample\": a ~22 min\nsuspend divided by `inner=33`) and poison mean/stdev untraced.\n\n- `saturating_record` replaces `record().unwrap()`: a sample\n  above the bound clamps at 60 s instead of panicking the run;\n  a clamp pileup stays visible in the `max` column.\n- Suspend detection (`ClockPair`): capture `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` +\n  `CLOCK_BOOTTIME` at run start; MONOTONIC freezes during\n  suspend, BOOTTIME keeps counting, so their elapsed divergence\n  is the suspended time. At ≥1 s the report prints a `WARNING`\n  naming the bench and gap; a clamped `max` prints a second\n  `WARNING` (covers a wedged sample with no suspend).\n- The 60 s bound is a sane-world ceiling, not a type limit\n  (u64 ns holds ~584 years); recording the \"true\" hours-long\n  value would only distort mean/stdev further, so clamp + flag\n  beats raising the bound.\n- We think minstant's TSC keeps counting across s2idle on the\n  3900X, which is why the sleep gap appeared in samples at all —\n  detection therefore uses std `Instant`, not minstant.\n\n# References\n\n[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/iiac-perf/commit/8aaccf8518c4 \"8aaccf8518c4cb46bcc2fbf96a317d5d4c962f68\""
}
```

> DEVELOPER

Neither of these changes are not related to zcr, don't conflate them. Do you agree?

> AGENT

Yes, I agree — on two counts, one of which I should have caught myself:

1. **The zcr bookkeeping I folded in.** I backfilled the zcr section's `Commits:` line (`[[6]]` for `de5dc57e`) and added the missing `[40]` definition inside this harness-fix commit. The protocol does say backfill rides "one push later" — but it doesn't say it must ride *inside* an unrelated change. A separate tiny notes-only commit (`chore: backfill zcr commit refs + [40] def`) keeps the harness fix pure. I'll split those edits out.

2. **The chores narrative leans on zcr.** The bug is a harness bug — any bench running while the machine suspends hits it. My section text opens with "Two long zcr runs..." and cites `zcr-with-1t`, which frames it as a zcr problem. The discovery context deserves maybe one sentence; the failure mechanism should be described bench-agnostically (a sample spanning a suspend; `inner=1` overflows the bound, `inner>1` poisons silently under it).

To be clear on scope after the split, the plan becomes three commits, each self-contained:

- `chore:` zcr bookkeeping only — `Commits:` backfill `[[6]]`, dangling `[40]` definition.
- `fix: saturate hist records, flag suspended runs` — harness.rs, Cargo.toml, README warning docs, its own chores section (rewritten bench-agnostic) + todo Done entry `[41]`.
- `docs:` the systemd-inhibit note.

Want me to restructure that way? (The working copy currently has everything mixed; I'd rewrite the chores narrative, then use the jj new/squash flow to carve out the bookkeeping commit first.)

> DEVELOPER

yes, restructure that way

> AGENT

One practical order note: the working copy currently *is* the harness fix, so I'll land it first, then the bookkeeping chore, then the docs commit — carving the bookkeeping in below it would just invite rebase conflicts on adjacent lines in todo.md for no benefit. Same three clean commits.

> AGENT

That config is empty apart from a migration marker — the machine is on Plasma 6 defaults, so there's no explicit timeout to read. The journal shows suspends landing 38–49 minutes after each resume, and the timer counts from your last keyboard/mouse input, not from when the bench starts. That leads to two answers:

**There is no reliably-shorter `-d`.** 300 and 600 didn't trigger it because of their own length — six benches back-to-back kept the process running 30–100 minutes of wall clock, long enough for the idle timer to fire mid-run. A single bench with a short `-d` finishes before the machine ever gets sleepy. To trigger it via genuine idle you'd need one bench still running when the timer fires, so something like `-d 3000` (50 min), hands off the keyboard, then wake the box when you return.

**But you don't need to wait for idle — suspend it manually.** `systemctl suspend` takes the identical s2idle path the idle timer takes, so it's the same real test in ~2 minutes:

```
iiac-perf zcr-spin-2t -d 60        # the bench that panicked before
# ~10s in, from another terminal (or Ctrl+Z is no good — separate terminal):
systemctl suspend
# leave it asleep > 60s, then wake it with a keypress
```

Expected on the new binary: the run survives (previously this panicked), the in-flight sample clamps, and the report is preceded by both flags —

```
WARNING zcr-spin-2t: ...: system suspended ~90.0s during the run; data invalid
WARNING zcr-spin-2t: ...: sample(s) clamped at the 60s histogram bound; data invalid
```

If you wake it in under 60 s you'd see only the suspend warning (gap too small to clamp at `inner=1`). A 1t bench under the same treatment shows only the suspend warning too, since the gap divides by `inner`≈33 — that's the silent-poisoning case the detector exists for.

Since a wake needs a physical keypress, you're the necessary human in the loop: want to run it yourself as above, or shall I start the bench and issue the `systemctl suspend` at the right moment — you just wake the box after a minute or so?

> AGENT

The test confirms it end-to-end — and the numbers cohere nicely: the max sample is 10.37 s while the detected sleep is ~8.4 s; the difference is the suspend/resume machinery itself (freezing devices, resume latency), which runs while `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` still ticks. Previously this run would have recorded that 10.4 s silently (it's under the 60 s bound); now it's flagged.

On placement: I agree, after the report is better. The failure mode of warning-first is exactly what your transcript shows — it's adjacent to the calibration banner, and in a six-bench run it sits above a 15-line table and scrolls out of mind by the time you're reading the numbers it invalidates. Warning-last is the final thing your eye lands on for that bench. Two details I'd keep:

- Print it before the blank line that separates reports, so it visually binds to its own bench rather than floating toward the next bench's header (the `WARNING <bench-name>:` prefix already disambiguates, but binding helps).
- Keep it on stdout, not stderr/log — if you redirect a run to a file, the invalidity flag must travel with the saved report.

Implementation-wise there's one wrinkle: the warning currently prints inside `run_adaptive`, but the report is printed by a separate `print_report` call in each bench's `run()`. Moving it after means plumbing the suspend gap across that boundary. The clean way is explicit: `run_adaptive` returns the gap as a fifth element and `print_report` takes it, printing warnings at the end of the table — a mechanical two-line touch in each of the ~20 bench files. The zero-churn alternative (a pending-warnings buffer in the harness that `print_report` drains) adds hidden state that leaks a warning into the wrong report if any bench ever calls `run_adaptive` without `print_report`, so I'd rather eat the mechanical diff. The pending "convert harness to probe-based measurement" todo will subsume this plumbing eventually anyway.

Want me to make that change now, folded into the still-uncommitted fix commit?
