> DEVELOPER

In the report the p99-max shows:
  p99-max       510 ns    10,368,319,487 ns    10,368,318,978 ns      3,033,409    4,137 ns    4,126 ns

That reads 3,033,409 loops that were between 510ns and 10s of course there was only one at 10s, what if we had a row that was p99.999-max, isn't the other data is actaully valid?

> AGENT

Your read is right, and it exposes both a reporting-granularity gap and an overstatement in my warning text.

**The band really is hiding the story.** `p99-max` there holds 3,033,409 samples (1% of 301M). The genuine content of that band is the ordinary scheduler tail — 510 ns up through a few ms of preemptions — plus exactly one 10.4 s suspend artifact, and the single artifact dictates the band's `last`, dominates its `mean` (4,137 ns), and poisons the whole-histogram mean/stdev. Finer boundaries would isolate it: with `p99.9`, `p99.99`, `p99.999` rows, 301M samples put ~3,000 samples in `p99.999-max`, and the artifact sits alone at the top of that row while `p99-p99.9` and `p99.9-p99.99` show the real tail structure. That's valuable for a latency tool regardless of suspends — tail structure is the point of the exercise. The band framework makes it a small change (extend `boundary_pcts`/`boundary_names`; empty bands already skip printing). One detail: the trimmed `mean min-p99` currently means "all bands except the last," so I'd anchor the trim at the p99 boundary explicitly to keep that metric's definition stable when more tail bands exist.

**And yes — most of the data is valid.** A ~10 s suspend corrupts the one in-flight sample plus a handful around it (the ~2 s of freeze/thaw machinery), all landing in the extreme tail. Everything else — every band below the tail, the percentile boundaries themselves (one sample out of 301M can't move even p99.999), and the trimmed min-p99 stats — is untouched. What's actually invalid: `max`, the untrimmed mean/stdev, and the top band's contents. So my `data invalid` wording overclaims; something like `max/mean/stdev poisoned` states precisely what died. The flag should still print — you want to know the run spanned a suspend — but it shouldn't condemn the whole report.

Proposal, keeping changes unconflated:

- **Pending fix commit** (uncommitted): soften the warning wording to name what's poisoned rather than declaring all data invalid. That text was born in this commit, so refining it here isn't conflation.
- **New separate commit**: `feat:` finer tail bands (`p99.9`/`p99.99`/`p99.999`), with the trim anchored at p99. Slots in before the docs commit.

Want me to do both?

> DEVELOPER

yes, do both

> AGENT

Wording tweak into the pending fix commit first; tail bands will follow as their own commit once this one lands (they both touch `harness.rs`, so stacking them cleanly means committing the fix first).

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/src/harness.rs",
  "old_string": "/// Print `WARNING` lines when the finished run's data is invalid:\n///\n/// - the system suspended during the run (clock divergence — a\n///   mid-sample suspend inflates that sample by the sleep gap,\n///   poisoning mean/stdev even under the histogram bound);\n/// - one or more samples clamped at [`HIST_HIGH_NS`] (a wedged or\n///   suspend-inflated sample with no detected suspend).\n///\n/// Called at the end of [`print_report`] so the flag is the last\n/// line of the bench's report, where it can't scroll out of mind.\nfn warn_invalid(name: &str, hist: &Histogram<u64>, suspended_s: f64) {\n    if suspended_s >= SUSPEND_WARN_S {\n        println!(\n            \"WARNING {name}: system suspended ~{suspended_s:.1}s during the run; data invalid\"\n        );\n    }\n    if !hist.is_empty() && hist.max() >= HIST_HIGH_NS {\n        println!(\n            \"WARNING {name}: sample(s) clamped at the {}s histogram bound; data invalid\",\n            HIST_HIGH_NS / 1_000_000_000\n        );\n    }\n}",
  "new_string": "/// Print `WARNING` lines when the finished run's tail-sensitive\n/// stats — `max` and the untrimmed mean/stdev — are poisoned:\n///\n/// - the system suspended during the run (clock divergence — a\n///   mid-sample suspend inflates that sample by the sleep gap,\n///   even under the histogram bound);\n/// - one or more samples clamped at [`HIST_HIGH_NS`] (a wedged or\n///   suspend-inflated sample with no detected suspend).\n///\n/// A few inflated samples out of millions land in the extreme\n/// tail band: percentile boundaries and the trimmed `min-p99`\n/// stats are unaffected, so the flag names what died rather than\n/// condemning the whole report. Called at the end of\n/// [`print_report`] so the flag is the last line of the bench's\n/// report, where it can't scroll out of mind.\nfn warn_invalid(name: &str, hist: &Histogram<u64>, suspended_s: f64) {\n    if suspended_s >= SUSPEND_WARN_S {\n        println!(\n            \"WARNING {name}: system suspended ~{suspended_s:.1}s during the run; max/mean/stdev poisoned\"\n        );\n    }\n    if !hist.is_empty() && hist.max() >= HIST_HIGH_NS {\n        println!(\n            \"WARNING {name}: sample(s) clamped at the {}s histogram bound; max/mean/stdev poisoned\",\n            HIST_HIGH_NS / 1_000_000_000\n        );\n    }\n}"
}

{
  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "/// A few inflated samples out of millions land in the extreme\n/// tail band: percentile boundaries and the trimmed `min-p99`\n/// stats are unaffected, so the flag names what died rather than\n/// condemning the whole report. Called at the end of\n/// [`print_report`] so the flag is the last line of the bench's\n/// report, where it can't scroll out of mind.\nfn warn_invalid(name: &str, hist: &Histogram<u64>, suspended_s: f64) {\n    if suspended_s >= SUSPEND_WARN_S {\n        println!(\n            \"WARNING {name}: system suspended ~{suspended_s:.1}s during the run; max/mean/stdev poisoned\"\n        );\n    }\n    if !hist.is_empty() && hist.max() >= HIST_HIGH_NS {\n        println!(\n            \"WARNING {name}: sample(s) clamped at the {}s histogram bound; max/mean/stdev poisoned\",\n            HIST_HIGH_NS / 1_000_000_000\n        );\n    }\n}",
  "new_string": "/// A few inflated samples out of millions land in the extreme\n/// tail band: percentile boundaries and the trimmed `min-p99`\n/// stats are unaffected, so the flag names what died rather than\n/// condemning the whole report. Called at the end of\n/// [`print_report`] so the flag is the last thing in the bench's\n/// report, where it can't scroll out of mind. Prints one\n/// `WARNING {name}:` header with each finding indented below it,\n/// keeping the findings visible next to the long bench name.\nfn warn_invalid(name: &str, hist: &Histogram<u64>, suspended_s: f64) {\n    let mut findings: Vec<String> = Vec::new();\n    if suspended_s >= SUSPEND_WARN_S {\n        findings.push(format!(\n            \"system suspended ~{suspended_s:.1}s during the run; max/mean/stdev poisoned\"\n        ));\n    }\n    if !hist.is_empty() && hist.max() >= HIST_HIGH_NS {\n        findings.push(format!(\n            \"sample(s) clamped at the {}s histogram bound; max/mean/stdev poisoned\",\n            HIST_HIGH_NS / 1_000_000_000\n        ));\n    }\n    if !findings.is_empty() {\n        println!(\"WARNING {name}:\");\n        for finding in &findings {\n            println!(\"  {finding}\");\n        }\n    }\n}"
}

> AGENT

Both of those are pre-existing layout bugs, incidentally — visible in your very first transcripts (`stdev 1,002,824 ns` sat 2 columns right back then too), so they belong in the report-focused commits, not the suspend fix:

- **stdev off by 2**: column widths are computed only from the band rows' rendered strings. The summary `stdev` (161,068) is wider than the widest band mean (1,731), and Rust's right-align pads to a *minimum* width — wider content just overflows, shifting that line right by the difference. Fix: include the summary strings (mean, stdev, trimmed mean/stdev, adjusted values) in the width computation.
- **`meanadjusted`**: same mechanism on the header row — the `adjusted` label (8 chars) is allotted `" ns" + gap + adj_w` = 7+`adj_w` columns, so when adjusted values are single-digit (`adj_w`=1) the label exactly fills the field and lands flush against `mean`. Fix: floor `adj_w` at 3, giving the label ≥2 spaces of separation and shifting the column right as you asked.

Since these are report-table fixes and the tail-bands feature also reshapes the table, my plan is: land the pending suspend commit as-is, then a `fix: report column widths` commit, then `feat:` tail bands, then the zcr bookkeeping chore, then the systemd-inhibit docs. Five commits, no conflation.

That makes this the moment to finalize the suspend commit. Proposed description:

**Title:** `fix: saturate hist records, flag suspended runs`

**Body:**
```
Long runs died at hist.record().unwrap() when the desktop
idle-suspended mid-run: a sample spanning the suspend measures
the whole sleep gap and can exceed the histogram's 60 s bound
(ValueOutOfRangeResizeDisabled); a sub-bound gap recorded
silently, poisoning max and the untrimmed mean/stdev.

- src/harness.rs
  - record_sample: saturating_record replaces record().unwrap()
  - ClockPair: CLOCK_MONOTONIC + CLOCK_BOOTTIME captured at run
    start; elapsed divergence = time suspended during the run
  - run_adaptive: returns suspended_s as a fifth tuple element
  - print_report: takes suspended_s; ends the report with
    warn_invalid — a WARNING header plus indented findings
    (suspend detected, samples clamped)
  - new consts HIST_LOW_NS / HIST_HIGH_NS / SUSPEND_WARN_S;
    unit tests for ClockPair and clamping
- src/benches/ (16 bench files): plumb suspended_s from
  run_adaptive through print_report
- README.md: document the WARNING lines and what stays valid
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: 0.13.0 -> 0.13.1
- notes/chores/chores-04.md: open section (empty Commits:)
- notes/todo.md: Done entry for the fix
```

> DEVELOPER

Why is this a "non-top commit", I don't see it that way?

> AGENT

Fair challenge — it's only a non-top commit under one particular plan I assumed without saying so: batching all five commits into a single `vc-x1 push` at the end. In that world only the batch's final commit gets stamped by the push wrapper, so the earlier ones need their `ochid:` hand-added at commit time. That's the case AGENTS.md's "pushed later as a non-top commit" rule covers.

But if we `vc-x1 push` each change as it lands — the default workflow, and I suspect your mental model — then every commit is the top (and only) commit of its own push, the wrapper injects its `ochid:` automatically, each push pairs with its own `.claude` commit, and nothing is hand-written. That's cleaner all around, and it also gives each chores section its backfill opportunity on the next push naturally.

So: shall I skip the bare `jj commit` and instead run `vc-x1 push` now for the suspend fix (that's a push — asking your approval for this specific one), then proceed the same way commit-by-commit through columns, bands, bookkeeping, and docs?

> DEVELOPER

yes, in general that's how we work, unless a step was not functional or the functionality was some how not undesired.

> AGENT

Understood — per-commit pushes are the norm. Checking the push wrapper's flags, then pushing the suspend fix.

```
vc-x1 0.67.0

Dual-repo commit+push+finalize in one resumable command.

Collapses today's manual Commit-Push-Finalize Flow into a
single subcommand with two interactive approval gates and a
state machine with persistent progress so interruptions can
resume without re-doing completed stages.

Stages: preflight (fmt/clippy/test) → review (approve diff)
→ message ($EDITOR / --title+--body, approve text) →
commit-app → commit-claude (skipped if clean) → bookmark-both
→ push-app → finalize-claude. Failures in commit-app /
commit-claude / bookmark-both roll both repos back via
`jj op restore` to the snapshot recorded before commit-app.
After push-app succeeds the remote boundary is crossed and
recovery is forward-only.

Non-interactive use: pass both --title and --body plus --yes
to skip the review gate. Saved state carries title/body
across resumes so only the first invocation needs them.

Usage: vc-x1 push [OPTIONS] [BOOKMARK]

Arguments:
  [BOOKMARK]
          Bookmark to advance in both repos (positional form of `--bookmark`).
          
          Accepting a positional lets the common case read as `vc-x1 push main`
          without the `--bookmark` ceremony; `--bookmark` is kept as an alias
          for scripts and for composition with other args. The two forms
          conflict if both supplied.

Options:
      --bookmark <BOOKMARK>
          Bookmark to advance in both repos (flag form; see positional)

  -V, --version
          Print the `vc-x1 X.Y.Z` banner as the first line, then continue. With
          no subcommand, prints the banner and exits.
          
          Replaces clap's auto-version (which would exit after printing): the
          banner now rides along with normal subcommand execution rather than
          gating it, so scripts can capture the version *and* the command's
          output in one invocation.

      --restart
          Clear any saved state file and start from stage 1

  -v, --verbose...
          Verbose output: -v debug, -vv trace

      --from <STAGE>
          Explicit stage to jump to (advanced / debug use)

          Possible values:
          - preflight:       Run fmt / clippy / test / install / retest
          - review:          Present diff for the first approval gate
          - message:         Compose / edit the commit message; present for
            second gate
          - commit-app:      Commit the app repo
          - commit-claude:   Commit the `.claude` session repo (skipped if
            empty)
          - bookmark-both:   Advance both bookmarks to `@-`
          - push-app:        `jj git push --bookmark <b> -R .`
          - finalize-claude: `vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <b>
            ...`

      --log <LOG>
          Log file path (captures all levels)

      --step
          Pause between every stage for an interactive approval gate

      --status
          Print where the saved state thinks we are and exit

      --recheck
          Re-run `preflight` even on resume (default: skip if last run
          succeeded)

      --no-finalize
          Stop before `finalize-claude` so it can be run manually

      --dry-run
          Print the exact commands for every stage without side effects

      --title <STR>
          Commit title (skip `$EDITOR` for the message stage)

      --body <STR>
          Commit body (skip `$EDITOR` for the message stage)

  -y, --yes
          Auto-approve interactive prompts (review gate, message-edit
          confirmation). Required when `--title` / `--body` aren't both supplied
          in non-interactive contexts

  -h, --help
          Print help (see a summary with '-h')
```

  "old_string": "    // Column widths from rendered strings.\n    let label_w = rows\n        .iter()\n        .map(|r| r.label.len())\n        .max()\n        .unwrap_or(0)\n        .max(\"stdev min-p99\".len());\n    let first_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.first.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let last_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.last.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let range_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.range.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let count_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.count.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let mean_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.mean.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let adj_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.adj_mean.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);",
  "new_string": "    // Whole-histogram and trimmed (min–p99, excluding the\n    // p99-max band) summary values, rendered before the width\n    // pass so the widths account for them — the untrimmed stdev\n    // is often wider than any band mean and would otherwise\n    // overflow its column, shifting its line right.\n    let hist_mean = hist.mean();\n    let hist_adj = (hist_mean - adj).max(0.0);\n    let hist_mean_s = fmt_commas_f64(hist_mean, 0);\n    let hist_adj_s = fmt_commas_f64(hist_adj, 0);\n    let hist_stdev_s = fmt_commas_f64(hist.stdev(), 0);\n\n    let trim_count: u64 = band_count[..n_bands - 1].iter().sum();\n    let trim = if trim_count > 0 {\n        let trim_sum: u128 = band_sum[..n_bands - 1].iter().sum();\n        let trim_mean = trim_sum as f64 / trim_count as f64;\n        let trim_adj = (trim_mean - adj).max(0.0);\n\n        // Variance: walk histogram buckets, include only non-tail bands.\n        let mut trim_var_sum = 0.0f64;\n        let mut trim_var_count = 0u64;\n        let mut cum = 0u64;\n        for iv in hist.iter_recorded() {\n            let value = iv.value_iterated_to();\n            let count = iv.count_at_value();\n            let mid_rank = (cum as f64 + count as f64 / 2.0) / sample_count as f64;\n            let idx = boundary_pcts[1..]\n                .iter()\n                .position(|&b| mid_rank < b)\n                .unwrap_or(n_bands - 1);\n            if idx < n_bands - 1 {\n                let diff = value as f64 - trim_mean;\n                trim_var_sum += diff * diff * count as f64;\n                trim_var_count += count;\n            }\n            cum += count;\n        }\n        let trim_stdev = if trim_var_count > 1 {\n            (trim_var_sum / trim_var_count as f64).sqrt()\n        } else {\n            0.0\n        };\n\n        Some((\n            fmt_commas_f64(trim_mean, 0),\n            fmt_commas_f64(trim_adj, 0),\n            fmt_commas_f64(trim_stdev, 0),\n        ))\n    } else {\n        None\n    };\n\n    // Column widths from rendered strings — band rows and the\n    // summary lines that print in the mean/adjusted columns.\n    let label_w = rows\n        .iter()\n        .map(|r| r.label.len())\n        .max()\n        .unwrap_or(0)\n        .max(\"stdev min-p99\".len());\n    let first_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.first.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let last_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.last.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let range_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.range.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let count_w = rows.iter().map(|r| r.count.len()).max().unwrap_or(0);\n    let mean_w = rows\n        .iter()\n        .map(|r| r.mean.len())\n        .chain([hist_mean_s.len(), hist_stdev_s.len()])\n        .chain(trim.iter().flat_map(|(m, _, s)| [m.len(), s.len()]))\n        .max()\n        .unwrap_or(0);\n    // The `adjusted` header (8 chars) spans mean's ` ns` + GAP +\n    // adj_w = 7 + adj_w columns; the floor of 3 keeps ≥ 2 spaces\n    // between the mean and adjusted headers when the adjusted\n    // values are narrow.\n    let adj_w = rows\n        .iter()\n        .map(|r| r.adj_mean.len())\n        .chain([hist_adj_s.len()])\n        .chain(trim.iter().map(|(_, a, _)| a.len()))\n        .max()\n        .unwrap_or(0)\n        .max(3);"
}

Summary values (whole-histogram\n  and trimmed mean/stdev) are now rendered before the width\n  pass and included in it.\n- The `adjusted` header (8 chars) spans mean's ` ns` + gap +\n  adj_w = 7 + adj_w header columns, so single-digit adjusted\n  values (adj_w = 1) left the label flush against `mean`\n  (`meanadjusted`). adj_w now floors at 3, keeping ≥ 2 spaces\n  of separation.\n\n# References\n\n[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/iiac-perf/commit/8aaccf8518c4 \"8aaccf8518c4cb46bcc2fbf96a317d5d4c962f68\""

> AGENT

Both fixes verified live: `stdev 270 ns` now sits flush in the mean column, and the header reads `mean  adjusted` with two spaces even for single-digit values. fmt/clippy/test clean, 0.13.2 installed. Diff: `src/harness.rs` (summary values rendered before the width pass and included in `mean_w`/`adj_w`; `adj_w` floored at 3; summary lines print the pre-rendered strings), version bump, chores section, todo entry.

Proposed description:

**Title:** `fix: report column widths`

**Body:**
```
Two pre-existing print_report alignment bugs, both from format
widths being minimums that wide content silently overflows: a
summary stdev wider than any band mean shifted its line right,
and single-digit adjusted values left the 8-char adjusted
header flush against mean (meanadjusted).

- src/harness.rs
  - compute whole-histogram + trimmed summary values and render
    them before the width pass; include them in mean_w/adj_w
  - floor adj_w at 3 so the adjusted header keeps >= 2 spaces
    from mean when adjusted values are narrow
  - print summary lines from the pre-rendered strings
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: 0.13.1 -> 0.13.2
- notes/chores/chores-04.md: open section (empty Commits:)
- notes/todo.md: Done entry for the fix
```

OK to push?

> AGENT

Right — my floor of 3 only rescued the collision case (1-digit values); with 3-digit adjusted values like this run, `adj_w` was already 3, so nothing moved. The gap between `mean` and `adjusted` headers was still just 2 spaces, visibly tighter than every other column pair (which get the full 4-space gap). Raising the floor to 5 gives the pair the same 4-space separation as the rest of the table and shifts the adjusted column right accordingly.

The `adjusted` column has moved now — the header pair gets the same 4-space gap as every other column (`mean    adjusted`), and the data shifted right with it. Validation clean, 0.13.2 reinstalled, chores text updated to describe the floor of 5.

Updated description (one line changed):

**Title:** `fix: report column widths`

**Body:**
```
Two pre-existing print_report alignment bugs, both from format
widths being minimums that wide content silently overflows: a
summary stdev wider than any band mean shifted its line right,
and narrow adjusted values left the 8-char adjusted header
crowding (or touching) mean.

- src/harness.rs
  - compute whole-histogram + trimmed summary values and render
    them before the width pass; include them in mean_w/adj_w
  - floor adj_w at 5 so mean/adjusted headers keep the full
    4-space gap every other column pair gets
  - print summary lines from the pre-rendered strings
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: 0.13.1 -> 0.13.2
- notes/chores/chores-04.md: open section (empty Commits:)
- notes/todo.md: Done entry for the fix
```

> AGENT

Right — aligning header ends to the units' end characters is the cleaner rule: every unit-bearing column's header shifts 3 right, `count` (unitless) stays put. Since this commit is now headers + widths, I'll retitle it `fix: report column alignment`.

id: toolu_015R27zD7NMKWBisAuFE8C6f
```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/src/harness.rs",
  "old_string": "    // Header row.\n    let first_col = INDENT.len() + label_w + 1 + first_w;\n    let last_gap = \" ns\".len() + GAP.len() + last_w;\n    let range_gap = \" ns\".len() + GAP.len() + range_w;\n    let count_gap = \" ns\".len() + GAP.len() + count_w;\n    let mean_gap = GAP.len() + mean_w;\n    let adj_gap = \" ns\".len() + GAP.len() + adj_w;",
  "new_string": "    // Header row. Each label right-justifies to the last\n    // character of its column's ` ns` unit; `count` is unitless\n    // and right-justifies to its digits.\n    const UNIT: usize = \" ns\".len();\n    let first_col = INDENT.len() + label_w + 1 + first_w + UNIT;\n    let last_gap = GAP.len() + last_w + UNIT;\n    let range_gap = GAP.len() + range_w + UNIT;\n    let count_gap = GAP.len() + count_w;\n    let mean_gap = GAP.len() + mean_w + UNIT;\n    let adj_gap = GAP.len() + adj_w + UNIT;"
}
```

Final description:

**Title:** `fix: report column alignment`

**Body:**
```
Pre-existing print_report alignment problems: format widths
are minimums, so a summary stdev wider than any band mean
shifted its line right, and narrow adjusted values left the
8-char adjusted header crowding (or touching) mean; headers
also right-justified to their column's digit end, 3 characters
left of where the column visually ends (' ns').

- src/harness.rs
  - compute whole-histogram + trimmed summary values and render
    them before the width pass; include them in mean_w/adj_w
  - floor adj_w at 5 so mean/adjusted headers keep 4 spaces of
    separation when adjusted values are narrow
  - right-justify each header to the last character of its
    column's ' ns' unit; unitless count stays on its digits
  - print summary lines from the pre-rendered strings
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: 0.13.1 -> 0.13.2
- notes/chores/chores-04.md: open section (empty Commits:)
- notes/todo.md: Done entry for the fix
```

hiding tail structure and letting a single outlier (e.g. a

p99.9 / p99.99 / p99.999 boundaries split the tail four ways so
rare outliers isolate in p99.999-max.

Nice, We know why the 2s one happened, I `systemctl suspend`d, but what about million or so other outliers?

> AGENT

Those are the genuine article — real OS and hardware noise on an unpinned desktop run, and they're exactly what this tool exists to characterize. Reading your band structure (my `-d 5` run, same shape as yours), each tier has a distinct signature:

- **`p99-p99.9`, ~150k samples at 495–665 ns**: too many to be OS events — that's one round-trip occasionally taking a few extra spin iterations. We think it's cache-coherence variance: the line carrying the ring index ping-pongs between cores, and occasionally a snoop/arbitration delay or SMT-sibling traffic makes the store propagate slower. Hardware-level jitter, not the kernel.
- **`p99.9-p99.99`, ~14k at 0.7–2.7 µs**: the right magnitude and rate for interrupt handling — timer ticks and device IRQs landing on the measuring or echoing core (~1.4k/s across two spinning threads is plausible tick+IRQ traffic).
- **`p99.99-p99.999`, ~1.5k at 2.7–11 µs**: softirq work and short involuntary preemptions.
- **`p99.999-max`, ~162 at 11–210 µs**: full deschedules — the scheduler gave the core to something else (desktop background work) for a timeslice. ~32/s across two unpinned threads on a busy desktop is unremarkable.

The magnitude attributions past the first band are inference from typical costs, not measured causality — but the structure supports it, and it's why the trimmed `min-p99` row exists: the mean answers "what does a round-trip cost," the tail bands answer "what does my machine do to me."

Two knobs you already have to test this: `--pin 0,1` (your own README data: p99.99 tightens ~4× when pinned — it removes migrations and some preemption), and a quieter machine. The remaining tail after pinning is mostly IRQs/SMIs, which need `isolcpus`/`nohz_full`/IRQ affinity to chase further — potential future todo if you want publication-grade tails.

Meanwhile, 0.13.3 (tail bands) is still awaiting your push approval — OK to push?

> AGENT

Yes on consistency, with one big caveat on "fastest": `--pin 0,12` on your 3900X is the **SMT-sibling pair** — logical CPUs 0 and 12 are the same physical core. That's why this run is so fast: the ring's cache lines live in the L1/L2 that both hyperthreads share, so a "cross-thread" hop never leaves the core. You're seeing 35 ns adjusted trimmed vs ~100 ns for two real cores (`--pin 0,1`, your earlier tier-comparison numbers). It's a legitimate configuration — some real systems do co-locate producer/consumer on siblings for exactly this reason — but it's measuring intra-core communication, not the cross-core case most deployments hit.

So for optimizing an algorithm:

- **Pinning buys you repeatability**, which is the thing that matters for A-vs-B: no migrations, stable cache topology, tight stdev (your `stdev min-p99` dropped to 3 ns — that's publication-grade consistency in the body of the distribution).
- **Which pinning to use depends on what you're optimizing for.** Hold it constant across variants and the *relative* comparison is valid at any tier. For *absolute* numbers, pick the topology matching deployment: `0,12` = shared-core best case; `0,1` = same-CCX cross-core through shared L3 (the usual realistic tier); on a 3900X you also have cross-CCX (`--pin 0,3`) and cross-CCD (`--pin 0,6`) tiers, each adding fabric latency. Running the same bench across those tiers is itself a useful experiment — it tells you how coherence-sensitive the algorithm is.
- Note the tail didn't disappear: 1,232 samples at 58–916 µs even pinned. Pinning constrains *your* threads, it doesn't reserve the core — the desktop still preempts you. The trimmed row dodges that; eliminating it needs core isolation.

One more thing your run shows: with the faster step (37 ns), the harness auto-sized `inner` to 3, so each sample now averages 3 round-trips — fine for the mean, but it dilutes single-call tail resolution; `-i 1` restores per-call sampling if you're studying the tail specifically.

And 0.13.3 is still queued on your approval — OK to push?

> AGENT

Good instinct with the power button — and your machine's state explains the asymmetry:

**Why the power button always works:** it isn't a peripheral. It's wired to the chipset as an ACPI *fixed event*, delivered over the ACPI SCI interrupt, and the spec requires it to be armed as a wake source in every sleep state — no driver, no USB stack, no configuration involved. Your `/sys/power/pm_wakeup_irq` reads `9`, the ACPI SCI, confirming the last wake came in exactly that way. A short press is just a wake event (it only becomes "power off" via a long ≥4 s press, which is a firmware hard-cut), so it was always the right thing to try before the reset button.

**Why the keyboard is unreliable:** a USB keystroke waking the box is the end of a long conditional chain — the keyboard must have remote-wakeup armed, every hub between it and the controller must be armed, the xHCI controller must arm PME before entering D3, and that PME must be routed to a wake-capable interrupt. Every link has to hold. On your box the links are actually *not* formally armed: all ten USB root hubs report `power/wakeup: disabled` (only the controller `XHC0` is enabled at the ACPI level). So keyboard wake on this machine was never guaranteed configuration-wise — we think the earlier suspends woke on keypress because the controller happened to still be in a responsive state when you pressed, and on the last cycle it had fully quiesced into D3 with no remote wake armed, leaving the keyboard electrically mute. AMD xHCI wake arming is also known to be flaky across repeated s2idle cycles, which fits your "several worked, then one didn't" pattern.

If you want keyboard wake to be dependable: `echo enabled | sudo tee /sys/bus/usb/devices/usbN/power/wakeup` for the hub chain your keyboard hangs off (made persistent with a udev rule). For bench purposes though, the power button is the robust wake path — and once the inhibit step lands, wake-from-suspend stops being part of the workflow at all.

> AGENT

That count is actually the design working, not a surprise in the data — `p99.999-max` holds the top 0.001% *by definition*, and 0.001% of 125.6M samples is ~1,256. Percentile bands scale with sample count, so that row will never have count 1 on a long run; a count of 1 would need an absolute row like "max only" (which the band's `last` column already gives you: 3.5 ms). What the row genuinely tells you: even pinned, cores 0/1 eat ~63 multi-µs stalls per second — pinning constrains your threads but doesn't reserve the cores, so desktop background work still preempts them, and 3.5 ms is a full scheduler timeslice lost. Getting that row toward zero is `isolcpus`/`nohz_full` territory.

> AGENT

Unwound — the working copy now carries just the backfills, the `[40]` repair, and the version bump, all of which will ride with the inhibit commit.

For that commit, the design choice I flagged earlier: how should inhibit work?

1. **Docs-only** — a README note: wrap long runs in `systemd-inhibit --what=sleep iiac-perf ...`. Zero code, but relies on remembering it, and the runs it saves are exactly the long unattended ones where you forgot.
2. **Built-in, automatic (my recommendation)** — at startup the binary re-execs itself under `systemd-inhibit --what=sleep` (guarded by an env var to prevent recursion; needs no new dependencies, just `std::os::unix::process::CommandExt::exec`). If `systemd-inhibit` isn't present (non-systemd box), it proceeds uninhibited with a note. Every run is protected, nothing to remember; the suspend detector stays as the backstop for the fallback path.
3. **Opt-in flag** (`--inhibit`) — same mechanism, but only when asked. Keeps default behavior untouched at the cost of the same "you must remember" weakness as docs-only.

A benchmark run of any length is invalidated by a suspend, so I'd make protection the default (option 2, with the README documenting it). Which shape do you want?

> DEVELOPER

> DEVELOPER

> AGENT

Yes, there are real reasons — you hit one of them today:

- **Testing the suspend machinery itself**: `--what=sleep` inhibitors block `systemctl suspend` too (logind refuses unless you pass `-i`). Reproducing today's WARNING tests would require `--no-inhibit` (or `systemctl suspend -i`).
- **Tracing/profiling wrappers**: the re-exec inserts `systemd-inhibit` into the exec chain, which complicates `strace`/`gdb`/`perf` sessions on the binary; `--no-inhibit` keeps the process image untouched.
- **Deliberate policy**: an unattended laptop where you'd rather the machine sleep on lid-close and lose the run than cook the battery overnight.

All niche, which is exactly the shape that justifies default-on with an escape hatch. Implementing now — let me look at main.rs for the CLI structure.

11	use clap::Parser;
12	use log::{debug, info};
13	
14	#[derive(Parser)]
15	#[command(
16	    version,
17	    about = concat!(
18	        "iiac-perf ",
19	        env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION"),
20	        " — Rust latency microbenchmark harness",
21	    ),
22	    max_term_width = 80,
23	)]
24	struct Cli {
25	    /// Benches to run. Pass 'all' for every registered bench, or
26	    /// one or more names; a name matching no bench exactly runs
27	    /// every bench it is a prefix of (e.g. 'ice', 'mpsc').
28	    /// Run with no args to see the available list.
29	    benches: Vec<String>,
30	
31	    /// Target wall-clock seconds per bench (default 5.0). Auto-sizes
32	    /// outer and inner loop counts. Mutually exclusive with -D.
33	    #[arg(short = 'd', long, conflicts_with = "total_duration")]
34	    duration: Option<f64>,
35	
36	    /// Target total wall-clock seconds across all requested benches;
37	    /// budget is split equally per bench. Mutually exclusive with -d.
38	    #[arg(short = 'D', long)]
39	    total_duration: Option<f64>,
40	
41	    /// Override outer loop count (skips auto-sizing; inner still adapts).
42	    #[arg(short, long)]
43	    outer: Option<u64>,
44	
45	    /// Override inner loop count (skips auto-sizing).
46	    /// inner=1 measures single-call latency (each sample = one step); higher
47	    /// inner measures back-to-back/burst rate (each sample = N steps averaged).
48	    #[arg(short, long)]
49	    inner: Option<u64>,
50	
51	    /// Pin bench threads to logical CPUs (comma-separated, ranges OK).
52	    /// The list is a core *pool*: thread `i` of a bench is pinned to
53	    /// `pool[i % pool.len()]`, so shorter pools oversubscribe by wrap.
54	    /// Examples: `--pin 0,1` (2 threads → 2 CPUs), `--pin 0-5` (6-thread
55	    /// pool), `--pin 0,0` (two threads on the same CPU). On 3900X,
56	    /// logical CPUs N and N+12 are SMT siblings of the same physical
57	    /// core — `--pin 0,12` pairs siblings (max contention), `--pin 0,1`
58	    /// gives independent cores. Omit to leave threads unpinned.
59	    #[arg(long, value_name = "CORES")]
60	    pin: Option<String>,
61	
62	    /// Skip pinning the main thread for calibration. Always takes
63	    /// effect, including when `--pin` is set — bench threads still
64	    /// pin per `--pin`, but main stays on whatever affinity mask
65	    /// the process launched with. By default (without this flag),
66	    /// calibration runs with main pinned to `pin[0]` if set, else
67	    /// core 0, so framing/loop come from a stable environment.
68	    /// Pass this flag to decouple the calibration environment from
69	    /// the bench pinning pool (or to reproduce pre-0.6.0 behavior
70	    /// when `--pin` is absent).
71	    #[arg(long)]
72	    no_pin_cal: bool,
73	
74	    /// Enable verbose internals on stderr: affinity mask, cal
75	    /// parameters and raw fit inputs, calibration wall time.
76	    /// Equivalent to `RUST_LOG=debug`. Default is `warn` (silent
77	    /// unless something's wrong). `RUST_LOG` overrides this flag
78	    /// when set, so per-module filtering still works.
79	    #[arg(short, long)]
80	    verbose: bool,
81	
82	    /// Show tprobe results in raw TSC ticks instead of converting
83	    /// to nanoseconds. Only affects `TProbe` output; `Probe`
84	    /// results are always in nanoseconds.
85	    #[arg(short = 't', long)]
86	    ticks: bool,
87	}
88	
89	const DEFAULT_DURATION: f64 = 5.0;
90	
91	fn main() {
92	    let cli = Cli::parse();
93	
94	    // Default filter is `warn`; `-v` bumps to `debug`. `RUST_LOG`
95	    // (if set) always wins — so users can still do fine-grained
96	    // per-module filtering without fighting the flag.
97	    let mut builder = env_logger::Builder::from_default_env();
98	    if std::env::var_os("RUST_LOG").is_none() {
99	        builder.filter_level(if cli.verbose {
100	            log::LevelFilter::Debug
101	        } else {
102	            log::LevelFilter::Warn
103	        });
104	    }
105	    builder.format_timestamp(None).init();
106	
107	    if cli.benches.is_empty() {
108	        println!("no benches specified. use -h or --help for more info.");
109	        println!("available: all, {}", benches::names().join(", "));
110	        return;
111	    }
112	
113	    println!(
114	        "iiac-perf {} — Rust latency microbenchmark harness\n",
115	        env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION")
116	    );
117	
118	    if let Some(mask) = pin::current_affinity() {
119	        info!("startup affinity: {}", pin::affinity_summary(&mask));
120	    }
121	
122	    let pin_cores: Vec<usize> = match cli.pin.as_deref() {
123	        None => Vec::new(),
124	        Some(spec) => match pin::parse_cores(spec) {
125	            Ok(v) => v,
126	            Err(e) => {
127	                eprintln!("error: --pin: {e}");
128	                std::process::exit(2);
129	            }
130	        },
131	    };
132	
133	    // Pin main for calibration. Always pinned by default so
134	    // framing/loop numbers come from a stable environment
135	    // regardless of --pin. --no-pin-cal restores pre-0.6.0
136	    // behavior (pin iff --pin given).
137	    //
138	    // When we're doing the cal-only pin (--pin absent, --no-pin-cal
139	    // absent), snapshot the pre-cal affinity and restore it after
140	    // calibrate() so the scheduler regains freedom to co-locate
141	    // bench threads — otherwise main's pin leaks into the
142	    // scheduler's placement decisions and suppresses the
143	    // "both ends hot and spinning" fast path.
144	    let cal_pin: Option<usize> = if cli.no_pin_cal {
145	        None
146	    } else {
147	        pin_cores.first().copied().or(Some(0))
148	    };
149	    // Save/restore pre-cal affinity whenever we're about to set a
150	    // cal pin that wasn't the user's explicit bench-pin request —
151	    // i.e. the cal-only pin (--pin absent, --no-pin-cal absent).
152	    // --pin ⇒ user already wants main on pin[0]; leave alone.
153	    // --no-pin-cal ⇒ we aren't pinning at all; nothing to save.
154	    let saved_affinity = if pin_cores.is_empty() && !cli.no_pin_cal {
155	        pin::save_affinity()
156	    } else {
157	        None
158	    };
159	    pin::pin_current(cal_pin);
160	
161	    if let Some(cpu) = cal_pin {
162	        info!("pinned main to core {cpu} for calibration");
163	    } else {
164	        info!("cal pin skipped");
165	    }
166	    if let Some(mask) = pin::current_affinity() {
167	        debug!("affinity during cal: {}", pin::affinity_summary(&mask));
168	    }
169	
170	    let amp_coeff = overhead::N_HIGH as f64 / (overhead::N_HIGH - overhead::N_LOW) as f64;
171	    info!(
172	        "calibration params: warmup={}, samples={}, N_LOW={}, N_HIGH={}, noise_amp={:.4}",
173	        overhead::CAL_WARMUP,
174	        overhead::CAL_SAMPLES,
175	        overhead::N_LOW,
176	        overhead::N_HIGH,
177	        amp_coeff,
178	    );
179	
180	    let overhead = overhead::calibrate();
181	
182	    debug!(
183	        "calibration raw: min_low={} ns, min_high={} ns",
184	        overhead.cal_min_low_ns, overhead.cal_min_high_ns,
185	    );
186	    debug!(
187	        "calibration fit: framing={:.4} ns, loop_per_iter={:.4} ns",
188	        overhead.framing_per_sample_ns, overhead.loop_per_iter_ns,
189	    );
190	    info!(
191	        "calibration wall time: {:.2} ms",
192	        overhead.cal_duration.as_secs_f64() * 1000.0
193	    );
194	
195	    if let Some(set) = saved_affinity.as_ref() {
196	        pin::restore_affinity(set);
197	    }
198	
199	    let cal_pin_display = match (pin_cores.first(), cli.no_pin_cal) {
200	        (_, true) => "none (--no-pin-cal)".to_string(),
201	        (Some(c), false) => format!("core {c} (from --pin)"),
202	        (None, false) => "core 0 (unpinned after cal; --no-pin-cal to skip)".to_string(),
203	    };
204	    println!("Calibration:");
205	    println!(
206	        "  framing/sample    {:>7} ns  (timer pair, two-point fit)",
207	        harness::fmt_commas_f64(overhead.framing_per_sample_ns, 2)
208	    );
209	    println!(
210	        "  loop/iter         {:>7} ns  (per inner-loop iteration)",
211	        harness::fmt_commas_f64(overhead.loop_per_iter_ns, 2)
212	    );
213	    println!("  cal pin           {cal_pin_display}");
214	    println!("  bench pin         {}", pin::plan_summary(&pin_cores));
215	    println!();
216	
217	    let runners = match benches::resolve(&cli.benches) {
218	        Ok(r) => r,
219	        Err(e) => {
220	            eprintln!("error: {e}");
221	            std::process::exit(2);
222	        }
223	    };
224	
225	    let target_seconds = match (cli.duration, cli.total_duration) {
226	        (Some(d), _) => d,
227	        (None, Some(t)) => t / runners.len() as f64,
228	        (None, None) => DEFAULT_DURATION,
229	    };
230	
231	    let cfg = harness::RunCfg {
232	        overhead: &overhead,
233	        target_seconds,
234	        outer_override: cli.outer,
235	        inner_override: cli.inner,
236	        pin_cores: &pin_cores,
237	        report_ticks: cli.ticks,
238	    };
239	
240	    for run in runners {
241	        run(&cfg);
242	    }
243	}
244	
```

{
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/iiac-perf/src/inhibit.rs",
  "content": "//! Automatic system-sleep inhibition: re-exec the process under\n//! `systemd-inhibit` so an idle-suspend can't poison a long bench\n//! run — a mid-sample suspend inflates that sample by the whole\n//! sleep gap (see the harness suspend detection, which remains\n//! the backstop when inhibition is off or unavailable).\n\nuse std::os::unix::process::CommandExt;\nuse std::process::Command;\n\nuse log::warn;\n\n/// Env var marking the re-exec'd child so it doesn't recurse.\nconst GUARD: &str = \"IIAC_PERF_INHIBITED\";\n\n/// Ensure the process holds a `systemd-inhibit --what=sleep` lock,\n/// re-exec'ing itself once under the wrapper if needed. Returns\n/// the status line for the startup banner:\n///\n/// - `--no-inhibit` passed → disabled by request;\n/// - guard env var present → active (we are the re-exec'd child,\n///   the wrapper holds the lock for our lifetime);\n/// - re-exec failed (`systemd-inhibit` absent, non-systemd box)\n///   → run continues uninhibited; suspend detection still flags\n///   a poisoned run.\npub fn ensure(no_inhibit: bool) -> String {\n    if no_inhibit {\n        return \"disabled (--no-inhibit)\".to_string();\n    }\n    if std::env::var_os(GUARD).is_some() {\n        return \"active (systemd-inhibit --what=sleep)\".to_string();\n    }\n    let exe = match std::env::current_exe() {\n        Ok(p) => p,\n        Err(e) => {\n            warn!(\"sleep inhibit unavailable (current_exe: {e}); continuing uninhibited\");\n            return \"unavailable (current_exe failed; run uninhibited)\".to_string();\n        }\n    };\n    let err = Command::new(\"systemd-inhibit\")\n        .arg(\"--what=sleep\")\n        .arg(\"--who=iiac-perf\")\n        .arg(\"--why=benchmark run in progress\")\n        .arg(\"--mode=block\")\n        .arg(exe)\n        .args(std::env::args_os().skip(1))\n        .env(GUARD, \"1\")\n        .exec();\n    // exec only returns on failure (e.g. systemd-inhibit absent).\n    warn!(\"sleep inhibit unavailable ({err}); continuing uninhibited\");\n    \"unavailable (systemd-inhibit failed; run uninhibited)\".to_string()\n}\n"
}
```

> AGENT

All verified end-to-end: the logind inhibitor shows `iiac-perf … sleep … block` while a bench runs, the banner reports `active` / `disabled (--no-inhibit)` correctly, and the lock is gone after exit. fmt/clippy/test clean, 0.13.4 installed.

This commit also carries the backfills and the `[40]` repair, per the no-backfill-only-commits rule. Proposed description:

**Title:** `feat: inhibit sleep during bench runs`

**Body:**
```
Idle-suspend poisoned two long runs before the suspend detector
existed; detection flags a poisoned run but prevention is
better. The binary now re-execs itself under systemd-inhibit
--what=sleep before any output, so the machine can't
idle-suspend while a bench runs. --no-inhibit opts out; where
systemd-inhibit is unavailable the run continues uninhibited
and the detector remains the backstop.

- src/inhibit.rs: new module — guard env var, re-exec via
  CommandExt::exec, banner status string
- src/main.rs: --no-inhibit flag; call inhibit::ensure after
  the bench-list early return; banner "sleep inhibit" line
- README.md: --no-inhibit flag docs; note runs inhibit sleep
  by default
- Cargo.toml, Cargo.lock: 0.13.3 -> 0.13.4
- notes/chores/chores-04.md
  - open section (empty Commits:)
  - backfill Commits: [[6]]-[[9]] (zcr close-out + the three
    sections the last three pushes made permanent)
- notes/todo.md: Done entry; define dangling [40]
```

OK to push?

> DEVELOPER

let's explore the long tail now, let's change > p99 nomenclature to use 10-N like nomenclature and to -1..-10, do that make sense?

> DEVELOPER

let's explore the long tail now, let's change > p99 nomenclature to use 10-N.
Something like p99 10^-01 .. -10^-10, or maybe you have a more succinct suggestion?

> AGENT

The tail bands are exceedance fractions, so exponent notation is the natural fit: `p99.9` ≡ top `1e-3` of samples, and it stays compact all the way down (`p99.99999999` vs `1e-10`). To explore "how long is the tail" I'll also extend the boundaries from today's `1e-5` down to `1e-10` — empty bands already skip, and populating `1e-10` needs ~10¹⁰ samples (a ~20+ min run at 110 ns/call, now safe thanks to inhibit).

One wrinkle: with `-` as the row separator, `1e-3-1e-4` is unreadable — the label needs a different joiner. Let me show the options:

> TOOL

> AGENT

`1e-3` is an **exceedance fraction**: the fraction of samples lying *above* the boundary. So `1e-3` = 10⁻³ = 0.001 → 0.1% of samples above it → exactly **p99.9** (99.9% below). The general mapping is `1e-N` ≡ percentile `100 × (1 − 10⁻N)`.

In that nomenclature **p99 is `1e-2`** — 10⁻² = 1% of samples above it. The full ladder:

| exceedance | percentile |
|---|---|
| 1e-2 | p99 |
| 1e-3 | p99.9 |
| 1e-4 | p99.99 |
| 1e-5 | p99.999 |
| … | … |
| 1e-10 | p99.99999999 |

Which raises a consistency question I sided with familiarity on: the boundary row where the notations meet currently reads `p99..1e-3` — I kept the `p99` name because it's the anchor everyone knows (and the trimmed stat is labeled `min-p99`). The purist alternative is renaming that boundary `1e-2` so the tail reads `1e-2..1e-3, 1e-3..1e-4, …` uniformly. Happy to switch if you prefer the uniform form — say the word. Meanwhile my validation run got cut off, so I'll rerun it when we continue.

> AGENT

Your arithmetic is exactly right, and my "p99 is 1e-2" was sloppy shorthand. The precise statement: **they're complements describing the same boundary from opposite sides.** A boundary value x splits the samples; `p99` names it by the fraction *below* (0.99, the CDF), while `1e-2` names it by the fraction *above* (0.01, the tail). Same point on the distribution, two coordinates related by `above = 1 − below`. So "p99 ≡ 1e-2" meant "these two labels denote the same boundary," not that the numbers are equal — as you say, 0.99 ≠ 0.01.

On the word: "exceedance" is real but field-jargon. `P(X > x)` goes by several names — **exceedance probability** in hydrology/extreme-value statistics (a "100-year flood" is the level with annual exceedance 1e-2), **survival function** in reliability/biostatistics, **CCDF** (complementary CDF) in networking/latency work. Latency papers usually plot tails as log-scale CCDFs, which is exactly the view our tail bands discretize — so the concept is standard for this exact use, even if the word is niche. It was news to me that it'd be news to you, which is itself a signal: if "exceedance" needs a decoder ring, the docs should lead with a plainer word.

Proposal: in the README and doc comments, call it the **tail fraction** — "labels above p99 give the fraction of samples above the boundary: `1e-3` = top 0.001 (≡ p99.9)" — with "(exceedance)" as a parenthetical for people who know the term. The labels themselves stay `1e-3..1e-4` either way.

Want me to make that wording change and rerun the validation that got interrupted, then show you a demo run before the commit description?

> AGENT

It's a fair instinct — mixed nomenclature is a seam, and seams cost readers. But "switch everything" forks into two quite different designs, and it's worth seeing both before choosing:

**B — same boundaries, tail-fraction labels everywhere.** The deciles become `9e-1, 8e-1, … 1e-1`, p1 becomes `9.9e-1`, p99 becomes `1e-2`:

```
  min..9.9e-1      60 ns ...     (was min..p1)
  9.9e-1..9e-1    100 ns ...     (was p1..p10)
  5e-1..4e-1      110 ns ...     (was p50..p60)
  1e-1..1e-2      129 ns ...     (was p90..p99)
  1e-2..1e-3      150 ns ...
  1e-3..1e-4      210 ns ...
```

Fully uniform, but the body labels fight intuition: `5e-1` for the median, `9.9e-1` as a 6-char oddball, and mid-table rows like `8e-1..7e-1` are linear steps wearing exponential clothing — the notation implies decades where there aren't any.

**C — redesign the ladder to be log-uniform in tail fraction.** Boundaries at `5e-1, 1e-1, 1e-2, … 1e-10`; the table becomes a discretized log-CCDF, which is exactly how latency tails are plotted in the literature:

```
  min..5e-1       110 ns ...    (bottom half)
  5e-1..1e-1      115 ns ...
  1e-1..1e-2      129 ns ...
  1e-2..1e-3      150 ns ...
  ...
  1e-10..max
```

One notation, one row per decade, and every step down the table is "10× rarer." The cost: the decile view of the body collapses to two rows, so structure like your 100/109/110/119/120 ns plateaus across p1–p90 disappears into one aggregated band.

**A — status quo (mixed).** My defense of it: the body and the tail genuinely have different natural coordinates — percentiles where mass is dense, tail fractions where you're counting decades of rarity — and the seam sits at p99, the one landmark everybody knows. Mixed units with a well-known boundary is common practice (Hz below 1 kHz, kHz above).

My recommendation, given your stated goal is tail exploration: **C**, with the trim label becoming `min..1e-2`. It's the most principled, reads uniformly, and the body detail it sacrifices is the least interesting part of the table — and if we ever miss the deciles, a `--deciles` flag or second table could restore them. B is the option I'd argue against: consistency of notation without consistency of meaning.

Which way do you want it — C, B, or keep the mixed A as already implemented?

> AGENT

The both-tails observation is a good one, and it actually points at the constraint that's been fighting us: we need a notation that (a) scales by decade, (b) maps to percentile intuition, and (c) works symmetrically on both ends. `1e-N` gives you (a) and half of (c) but fails (b) — as you said, it doesn't *feel* like a percentile. Pure percentiles give (b) but fail (a). C gives (a) and elegance but fails (b).

One note on the fast tail before the proposal: it's physically different from the slow tail. Nothing can make a round-trip *faster* than its fast path — the distribution has a hard floor — so the low side is compressed against it (your `min..p1` spans 4→4 ns). The 329-sample scatter you're seeing is also partly apparatus: 1 ns timer quantization and `inner=32` averaging smear the true per-call values. So the low tail deserves *some* resolution (a fast outlier usually means a measurement artifact worth knowing about), but it'll never need ten decades the way the slow side might.

Here's a synthesis that I think satisfies all three constraints — **nines/zeros notation**, straight out of SRE-speak where "four nines" already means 99.99%:

- `nK` = K nines = boundary with 10⁻ᴷ of samples *above* it (`n2` = p99, `n3` = p99.9, `n10` = ten nines)
- `zK` = K zeros = boundary with 10⁻ᴷ of samples *below* it (`z2` = p1, `z3` = p0.1) — the mirror image

```
                first     ...
  min..z4        4 ns     (fastest 1e-4)
  z4..z3         4 ns
  z3..z2         4 ns
  z2..p10        4 ns     (z2 ≡ p1)
  p10..p20       ...      deciles unchanged
  p90..n2        6 ns     (n2 ≡ p99)
  n2..n3         7 ns
  n3..n4         9 ns
  n4..n5       135 ns
  ...
  n10..max
```

Why I like it: labels stay 2–3 characters at *any* depth (`n10` vs `p99.99999999` vs `1e-10`); "n3 = three nines" is a mental mapping people already carry, unlike exceedance fractions; and the low side gets a symmetric, equally-compact vocabulary instead of `p0.0001`. The deciles keep their familiar names, and the seams (`z2`, `n2`) sit exactly at p1/p99 where the old table already had joints. Cost: one sentence of decoder ring in the README — "nK/zK = K nines/zeros" — which I'd argue is cheaper than any of the alternatives' costs.

The two candidates standing, then: **nines/zeros symmetric** (above), or **keep the mixed `1e-N` version** that's currently implemented and uncommitted. I'd go nines/zeros — it's the only scheme where your own phrase "how many nines deep is this outlier?" is literally the label. Your call, and if you want it, I'd give the low side `z2..z4` to start (given the floor compression) and the high side `n2..n10`.

> DEVELOPER

First provide me a link (or ..) which we can have in our documenation explaining this. I like the idea of keeping the "p's" in the middle which people will likely be familiar with.

p1 = 0.01 = z2. So zero count is one to left of decimal and one to the right. But n's are counting 9's only to the right of the decimal because there's a zero to the left, am I confused?

> AGENT

You're not confused — you've caught that the naive digit-count mnemonic is asymmetric, and it's worth getting the definition crisp for the docs.

The clean, symmetric definition is by **exponent**, not digit-counting: `nK` and `zK` mark the boundary where a fraction **10⁻ᴷ** of samples lies on that side — above for `n`, below for `z`. So `n2`: 10⁻² above → CDF 0.99; `z2`: 10⁻² below → CDF 0.01. Same K, mirrored.

The digit intuition then falls out, but you have to phrase it as *position*, not count: the significant digit sits in the **K-th decimal place** — `nK` puts its last 9 there (0.9**9**, 0.99**9**), `zK` puts its 1 there (0.0**1**, 0.00**1**). "Two nines" happens to also count digits for `n` because 1−10⁻² renders as two 9s; for `z` the count-reading only works if you include the integer-part zero, which is exactly the wobble you noticed. Docs should define by 10⁻ᴷ and offer "K nines" as the mnemonic on the slow side.

For a citable link — "nines" is standard engineering usage; let me verify the reference before we put it in the docs:

# Nines (notation) - Article Exists

Yes, this Wikipedia article exists and defines the "nines" notation system.

## Core Definition

The article explains that **nines represent an informal logarithmic notation for proportions near 100%**. As stated: "Nines are an informal logarithmic notation for proportions very near to one or, equivalently, percentages very near 100%."

## Coverage

The article comprehensively covers all three applications you mentioned:

1. **Purity of materials** — particularly precious metals (gold, silver, platinum) and industrial gases, measured using millesimal fineness

2. **System availability** — describing computer uptime/downtime (e.g., "five nines" equals 99.999% uptime, meaning unavailability of roughly 26 seconds monthly)

3. **The notation itself** — how consecutive nines in percentages (99%, 99.9%, 99.99%) are counted as "two nines," "three nines," "four nines," etc.

The mathematical formula provided is: **nines = −log₁₀(1 − x)**, where x represents the proportion.
```

The slow tail subdivides down to `n10`, the\nfast tail only to `z4` — a latency distribution is floored below\n(nothing beats the fast path) and open above. There's your count of one — the table now runs the tail dry: this 20 s run resolves eight decades, ending with `n8..n9` holding exactly 1 sample (the single 256 µs worst case), and each decade above it shows the tail's actual shape (`n3..n4` jumps 220 ns → 4.3 µs — that's where IRQ costs enter; `n6..n7` is the preemption zone). The fast tail shows structure too: 15.8k samples at 50–80 ns *below* the 100 ns body — faster-than-normal round-trips worth understanding someday. Validation clean, 0.13.5 installed.

**Body:**
```
Exploring the tail needs bands beyond p99.999; percentile names
grow a digit per decade and bare exceedance fractions don't
read as percentiles. Both tails now use nines/zeros notation -
nK/zK mark the boundary with a fraction 10^-K of samples above
(n) / below (z), so n2 = p99, n3 = p99.9, ... n10 and z2 = p1 -
while the body keeps familiar deciles ("K nines" per Wikipedia
Nines (notation); zK is our mirror for the fast tail).

- src/harness.rs
  - boundary_pcts/boundary_names: z4/z3/z2 fast-tail bands,
    slow tail extended n2..n10
  - row label separator - -> .. everywhere (p40..p50, n3..n4)

```
let boundary_pcts: &[f64] = &[
        0.0,
        0.0001,

        0.10,
        0.20,

```

> AGENT

That's the most self-documenting option — the label *is* the definition — but it re-imports the exact problem that started this: deep labels are only distinguishable by counting nines by eye. Compare the same rows:

```
  n8..n9            255,999 ns ...
  0.99999999..0.999999999            255,999 ns ...

  n9..n10
  0.999999999..0.9999999999
```

At `0.99999999` vs `0.999999999` the difference is one glyph in a run of ten identical ones — that's the "doesn't scale well" complaint you made about `p99.999`, amplified. Humans can't subitize past ~4 repeated digits, which is *why* the "five nines" idiom exists: `nK` is literally the pre-counted form of these fractions. The label column would also grow from 13 to ~24 chars, pushing an already-wide table further right.

Where I think the literal values genuinely belong is the **documentation layer** rather than the labels: the README's mapping table (`n2` = 0.99, `z4` = 0.0001, …) so a reader can always dereference, and — if you want it in the tool itself — a `--explain-bands`-style dump or a footnote line under the table could print the label→fraction mapping on demand. There's also a small code tidy available in the same spirit: the `boundary_names` array could be *generated* from `boundary_pcts` (deciles → `pNN`, tails → `nK`/`zK` via `-log10`), making the fractions the single source of truth and the names derived — the literalness lives in the code, the readability in the output.

My recommendation: keep `nK`/`zK` labels, and if you'd like, I'll add the label↔fraction mapping table to the README (one commit, tiny). Want that, or do you prefer the literal labels despite the counting problem?

> DEVELOPER

I'm thinking single column

> AGENT

Single column reads well — label each row by its **upper boundary**, with the lower boundary implied by the row above. That's actually semantically cleaner with skipped empty bands too (a row's count is "samples since the last printed boundary"). It also halves the width problem, which makes your literal-fractions idea more viable, especially with one more trick: digit grouping. Two candidate shapes:

**A — single column, `nK`/`zK` labels:**
```
            first          last         range         count       mean
  z4         50 ns         80 ns        31 ns        15,781      79 ns
  z3         89 ns         99 ns        11 ns        29,568      90 ns
  p10       100 ns        109 ns        10 ns    15,546,373     107 ns
  p50       110 ns        110 ns         1 ns    88,934,258     110 ns
  p90       119 ns        120 ns         2 ns    12,704,451     120 ns
  n2        129 ns        160 ns        32 ns     7,111,356     139 ns
  n3        170 ns        210 ns        41 ns       870,261     178 ns
  n9        255,999 ns    ...
```

**B — single column, literal fractions with `_` grouping** (grouping solves the count-the-nines problem: `0.999_999_999` = three groups of three = n9, no squinting):
```
                    first          last  ...
  0.000_1            50 ns         80 ns
  0.001              89 ns         99 ns
  0.10              100 ns        109 ns
  0.50              110 ns        110 ns
  0.90              119 ns        120 ns
  0.99              129 ns        160 ns
  0.999             170 ns        210 ns
  0.999_999_999     255,999 ns
```

B is fully self-documenting — no notation to learn, the label is the number, and grouping makes depth legible. Its costs: the label column is ~13 chars vs 3 (wider table), and you lose the spoken-form mapping ("three nines") that `nK` gives you for free. A is the tersest and keeps the SRE vocabulary; its cost is the one-line decoder ring.

I'd take **A**, with the README carrying the label→fraction table so the literal values are one glance away. But B is a respectable choice if you want the output to need zero explanation. Which one?

> AGENT

Wishy-washy by option is legitimate here — the table *is* the product, and display preferences genuinely differ by audience. Thoughts on each piece:

**Label-style option.** `--band-labels zpn|frac|both` feels right (`frac` with the `_` grouping). Your learning-curve argument suggests defaulting to `both` — the juxtaposition `n3  0.999` teaches the mapping every time you look at it, and graduates set `zpn` in config and get the terse table. One design guard: every display option we add is a point in output-format space that lands in chores/READMEs, so recorded results should ideally state the style used (the header line could carry `labels=zpn`). Sequencing thought: the pending 0.13.5 ships range labels (`n2..n3`) that single-column would immediately replace — rather than land churn, I'd fold **single-column zpn** into the uncommitted work now (it's a small delta from what's built) and do the `--band-labels` option as its own cycle after config exists.

**Config file.** Agreed, and it's the prerequisite that keeps the option approach from becoming flag soup. Shape: `~/.config/iiac-perf/config.toml` (XDG), maybe a project-local override, CLI always wins. Costs a serde+toml dependency — fine. The sleeper feature for your machine: **named pin profiles** — `pin.smt = "0,12"`, `pin.ccx = "0,1"`, `pin.ccd = "0,6"` — so topology experiments become `--pin ccx`. Also natural homes for default duration, label style, decimals.

**Decimals on time columns.** Two distinct tiers, worth keeping separate:

- *Cheap and real:* the mean/adjusted columns are already f64 — showing 1–2 decimals is one formatting change and is honest precision (adjusted subtracts a fractional overhead like 11.61 ns, so `98 ns` is genuinely `98.4`-ish today). Could be `--decimals N` / config.
- *Deeper:* first/last/range are integer-ns *recorded* values — decimals there would be invented precision unless we change the recording unit. The real fix is recording in **picoseconds** (hdrhistogram is unit-agnostic; bounds become 1..6e13, a few more buckets, trivial memory). That would give 1t benches true sub-ns resolution — right now a 4.7 ns per-call rounds to 5 and your `inner=33` division truncates real signal. Worth its own todo; it touches `round_elapsed`, the 60 s clamp, and display scaling, so it's a contained but genuine cycle.

Proposed order: fold single-column into pending 0.13.5 → land it → todos for `--band-labels` option, config file, decimals-display, picosecond recording (config before the option; decimals-display any time; ps-recording independent).

> AGENT

All three pieces working in one screenshot: the inhibitor blocks `systemctl suspend` with a message naming `iiac-perf` and the reason; `--no-inhibit` restores testability exactly as intended; and the single-column table isolates the worst sample alone in `n9`. The fast tail is visible too — unpinned, you get 9,448 samples at 20–30 ns (`z4`), which is *faster* than the 110 ns body; those are worth an investigation of their own someday.

One anomaly worth flagging before we celebrate completely: **the suspend gap isn't in the histogram.** The detector measured ~1.2 s suspended *inside the measured window*, so one in-flight sample should have absorbed roughly that gap — yet the table's max (`n9`) is only 4.0 ms. Compare your 0.13.1 test: 8.4 s detected, and the max sample was 10.4 s — there the gap showed up as expected. Today it vanished from the samples while the BOOTTIME/MONOTONIC detector still saw it. Possibilities I can't yet distinguish: the TSC (which `minstant` uses for per-sample timing) halted across *this* suspend but counted through the earlier one, or the kernel froze the threads at a point that somehow fell outside the timed region. The two runs disagree about how the clocks behave across s2idle, which is exactly the kind of thing this tool exists to pin down. A repeat of the `--no-inhibit` + suspend test comparing "detected gap" vs "max sample" would discriminate — if max ≈ gap + ~2 s machinery, TSC counted; if max stays ms-scale, TSC halted and per-sample timing silently loses suspend time (worth a note in the docs either way).

That's a follow-up investigation though, not a blocker — the warning fired correctly, which is the safety net's job. Want me to push 0.13.5 as described, and add the "suspend gap vs max sample discrepancy" investigation as a todo (or a bugs.md entry) riding in it?

AGENTS.md:349:(bare `[N]`, or doubled `[[N]]`) never reuses another file's number; to cite a target a sibling
AGENTS.md:351:(the same target may carry a different number in each file). A
AGENTS.md:357:`[that section](../chores-07.md#…)` — not a bare number.
AGENTS.md:370:`chores-NN.md`: top to bottom) and number refs as their first
AGENTS.md:404:numbers in the source. For `## Todo` the number is its
AGENTS.md:394:### Todo format

> AGENT

It's already documented — the file just doesn't conform, and I compounded it by matching the file's style instead of the spec. The findings:

- **AGENTS.md [Todo format] (line 404)** is normative: "`## Todo` and `## Bugs` entries carry explicit `1.` `2.` … numbers in the source" — the number is the priority rank, `vc-x1 fix-todo --no-dry-run` renumbers, and durable references go by title, not number.
- **cycle-protocol.md** doesn't restate it, and I'd argue it shouldn't — it already defers with "pick up a `## Todo` item (typically the top-ranked, #1)", and AGENTS.md is the single source of truth for notes conventions. Duplicating the rule creates drift risk.
- **The real bug is `notes/todo.md` itself**: every entry is a `-` bullet, so `vc-x1 validate-todo` reports "0 entries checked" — the tooling has been validating nothing all along. That's also why the nonconformance survived: the validator can't flag entries it doesn't recognize.
- Two adjacent nits in the same area: AGENTS.md's own "Example shape" block shows `- ` dashes under `# Todo` (contradicting its normative text four paragraphs earlier), and `notes/todo.md` line 47 says "See [Foramt details](README.md#todo-format)" — typo'd and pointing at a README anchor that doesn't exist; the details live at `AGENTS.md#todo-format`.

Proposed small commit (`fix: number todo entries per AGENTS todo format`): convert the `## Todo` entries to `1.` … `N.` (keeping current order as the ranking — the five new ones stay on top unless you want to re-rank while we're at it), run `vc-x1 fix-todo --no-dry-run` + `validate-todo` to confirm the tooling now sees them, fix the AGENTS example block, and repoint the todo.md format link. Want me to do that — and any re-ranking preferences while the list is open?

13	- iiac-perf config file — `~/.config/iiac-perf/config.toml`
14	  (XDG), optional project-local override, CLI always wins;
15	  serde+toml deps. Homes for: named pin profiles
16	  (e.g. smt=0,12 / ccx=0,1 / ccd=0,6), default duration, band
17	  label style, decimals. Prerequisite for `--band-labels`.
18	- `--band-labels zpn|frac|both` — band label style option;
19	  `frac` = literal fractions with `_` grouping (`0.999_999`);
20	  default `both` for learnability; header line records the
21	  style in use so saved outputs are self-describing.
22	- Decimals on mean/adjusted columns (`--decimals N` + config) —
23	  cheap display change, the values are already f64.
24	- Record histogram in picoseconds — true sub-ns
25	  first/last/range resolution (matters for 1t benches where
26	  inner-division truncates today); touches `round_elapsed`,
27	  the 60 s clamp constant, and display scaling.
28	- Investigate: suspend gap missing from samples. A 0.13.5
29	  `--no-inhibit` suspend test detected ~1.2 s suspended inside
30	  the measured window but the max sample was only 4.0 ms,
31	  while the 0.13.1 test (8.4 s gap) showed the expected 10.4 s
32	  max sample. We think minstant's TSC may halt across some
33	  suspends and count through others. Repeat the test comparing
34	  detected gap vs max sample; if the TSC halts, per-sample
35	  timing silently loses suspend time — document either way.
36	- CLAUDE.md governance model (design cogitation) [20]
37	- Add framing adjustment to `Probe::report` (subtract
38	  `Overhead::framing_per_sample_ns` ≈ 11 ns in an `adjusted`
39	  column, mirroring `harness::print_report`)
40	- Convert `harness` / `Bench` to probe-based measurement. Will
41	  likely need inner-loop support on `Probe` (batch N calls per
42	  sample; report divides by N and accounts for per-sample
43	  framing) so very-small workloads can still amortize timer
44	  overhead the way `run_adaptive` does today.
45	- Rename app
46	- Design an app to measure IIAC perforanace written in Rust[1]
47	- `ice-ps-2t-wait` — iceoryx2 pub/sub with blocking waits via
48	  `Listener`/`Notifier` events; completes the {transport} ×
49	  {wait policy} matrix cell that compares against `mpsc-2t`
50	- Switch ice benches to the loan-based zero-copy send path
51	  (`loan_uninit` + `send`) — the API a perf-sensitive user would
52	  use, and closer to iceoryx2's own benchmark method
53	- Payload-size sweep for the round-trip benches (8 B / 8 KiB /
54	  1 MiB) — makes iceoryx2's size-independent latency vs channel
55	  copy cost visible in our own tables
56	- `crossbeam-1t` / `crossbeam-2t` — `crossbeam-channel` directly

58	- `tokio-mpsc-1t` / `tokio-mpsc-2t` — `tokio::sync::mpsc` round-trip

> DEVELOPER

I vote #2 first I want to see how that looks and maybe in the same #3 and #4, then we add the config afterwards as these need it, thoughts only?

> AGENT

That ordering works, and it fixes a flaw in my "config first" reasoning: config only stores *preferences*, and you can't know which preferences you want until the options exist and you've looked at their output. Evaluate first, persist later. Thoughts on the pieces:

**#2 first, CLI-only** — right call. `--band-labels zpn|frac|both` costs nothing without config; it just means typing the flag while experimenting, which is exactly the evaluation mode you want. Default `both` per our earlier discussion, so bare runs show the mapping while you're forming an opinion. Two details worth locking in when we build it: the header metadata line should record `labels=zpn` so saved outputs are self-describing, and `frac` gets the `_` grouping (`0.999_999_999`) so deep tails stay countable.

**#3 in the same cycle** — natural pairing; both are display options flowing through the same `RunCfg`→`print_report` plumbing, so the second commit rides the rails the first lays.

**#4 in the same cycle** — I was initially going to argue for separating it (it's a measurement-layer change, not display), but there's a real coupling that supports your bundling: `--decimals` without ps-recording is a half-feature. Mean/adjusted are f64 and can show decimals today, but first/last/range are integer-ns *recorded* values — decimals there would be invented precision until the recording unit is picoseconds. Doing #4 in the same cycle makes `--decimals` uniform across all columns instead of only some. One physics note for expectations: ps-recording adds real precision only where `inner > 1` — the timer itself reads integer ns, so sub-ns signal comes from averaging N calls per sample (exactly the 1t benches where 4.7 ns currently rounds to 5). At `inner=1` nothing changes.

So the shape would be this session's first true multi-commit ladder: `X.Y.Z-1` band-labels, `-2` decimals, `-3` ps-recording, close-out — with #4 getting a deliberate before/after bench comparison in its step since it can shift bucket boundaries. **Config (#1) afterwards**, by which point we'll know precisely which knobs earned config keys (label style and decimals for sure; probably default duration and named pin profiles while we're in there).

The todo re-rank (config drops below the three it serves) happens at cycle prep, with `fix-todo` renumbering. Ready to open the cycle whenever you say go.

296	}

298	/// Print the full bench report: header line (logfmt-style metadata),
299	/// per-band histogram, whole-histogram mean/stdev, and trimmed
300	/// mean/stdev (min..n2, excluding every band at or above n2 ≡ p99).
301	///
302	/// Each histogram row is one band, labeled by its **upper**
303	/// boundary — deciles in the body (`p10` … `p90`), nines/zeros in
304	/// the tails (`zK`/`nK` = fraction 10^-K of samples below/above
305	/// the boundary) — the lower boundary being the previous printed
306	/// row (empty bands are skipped). The `adjusted` columns
307	/// subtract per-call apparatus overhead
308	/// (`overhead.per_call_ns(inner)`); the untrimmed `stdev` is the
309	/// hdrhistogram-native stdev, which includes the ms-scale outliers
310	/// in the tail band. Ends with `WARNING` lines flagging poisoned
311	/// stats when they apply — `suspended_s` comes from
312	/// [`run_adaptive`] (see [`warn_invalid`]).
313	pub fn print_report(
314	    name: &str,
315	    outer: u64,
316	    inner: u64,
317	    duration_s: f64,
318	    hist: &Histogram<u64>,
319	    overhead: &Overhead,
320	    suspended_s: f64,
321	) {
322	    // Header line: bench name + logfmt-style metadata. `adj` is the
323	    // apparatus overhead subtracted from each sample downstream.
324	    let adj = overhead.per_call_ns(inner);
325	    let total = outer * inner;
326	    println!(
327	        "{name} [duration={:.1}s outer={} inner={} calls={} adj/call={}ns]:",
328	        duration_s,
329	        fmt_commas(outer),
330	        inner,
331	        fmt_commas(total),
332	        fmt_commas_f64(adj, 2),
333	    );
334	
335	    // Band boundaries defined by percentiles. Each consecutive pair
336	    // forms one band; we iterate the histogram to compute per-band
337	    // stats (first, last, count, mean).
338	    // Band boundaries: familiar deciles in the body, nines/zeros
339	    // notation in both tails — `nK`/`zK` mark the boundary with a
340	    // fraction 10^-K of samples above (n) or below (z), so
341	    // n2 ≡ p99, n3 ≡ p99.9, … n10, and z2 ≡ p1, z3 ≡ p0.1, z4.
342	    // "K nines" is standard engineering shorthand for proportions
343	    // near one (nines = -log10(1-x)); zK is this project's mirror
344	    // of it for the fast tail. The slow tail goes deeper because a
345	    // latency distribution is floored below (nothing beats the
346	    // fast path) and open above. Deep bands populate only when the
347	    // run has enough samples; empty bands are skipped.
348	    let boundary_pcts: &[f64] = &[
349	        0.0,
350	        0.0001,
351	        0.001,
352	        0.01,
353	        0.10,
354	        0.20,
355	        0.30,
356	        0.40,
357	        0.50,
358	        0.60,
359	        0.70,
360	        0.80,
361	        0.90,
362	        0.99,
363	        0.999,
364	        0.9999,
365	        0.99999,
366	        0.999999,
367	        0.9999999,
368	        0.99999999,
369	        0.999999999,
370	        0.9999999999,
371	        1.0,
372	    ];
373	    let boundary_names: &[&str] = &[
374	        "min", "z4", "z3", "z2", "p10", "p20", "p30", "p40", "p50", "p60", "p70", "p80", "p90",
375	        "n2", "n3", "n4", "n5", "n6", "n7", "n8", "n9", "n10", "max",
376	    ];
377	
378	    // Trim anchor: bands at or above the p99 boundary are the
379	    // "tail" — excluded from the trimmed min..n2 stats no matter
380	    // how many finer tail bands subdivide them.
381	    #[allow(clippy::unwrap_used)]
382	    // OK: 0.99 is a literal member of boundary_pcts above
383	    let trim_bands = boundary_pcts.iter().position(|&b| b == 0.99).unwrap();
384	
385	    let n_bands = boundary_pcts.len() - 1;
386	    let sample_count = hist.len();
387	
388	    // Accumulate per-band stats by walking recorded histogram buckets.
389	    // Each bucket is assigned to the band containing its midpoint rank.
390	    let mut band_first = vec![u64::MAX; n_bands];
391	    let mut band_last = vec![0u64; n_bands];
392	    let mut band_count = vec![0u64; n_bands];
393	    let mut band_sum = vec![0u128; n_bands];
394	
395	    let mut cumulative = 0u64;
396	    for iv in hist.iter_recorded() {
397	        let value = iv.value_iterated_to();
398	        let count = iv.count_at_value();
399	        let mid_rank = (cumulative as f64 + count as f64 / 2.0) / sample_count as f64;
400	        let idx = boundary_pcts[1..]
401	            .iter()
402	            .position(|&b| mid_rank < b)
403	            .unwrap_or(n_bands - 1);
404	        band_first[idx] = band_first[idx].min(value);
405	        band_last[idx] = band_last[idx].max(value);
406	        band_count[idx] += count;
407	        band_sum[idx] += value as u128 * count as u128;
408	        cumulative += count;
409	    }
410	
411	    // Build rendered rows: (label, first, last, range, count, mean, adj_mean).
412	    struct BandRow {
413	        label: String,
414	        first: String,
415	        last: String,
416	        range: String,
417	        count: String,
418	        mean: String,
419	        adj_mean: String,
420	    }
```
